\chapter{Certificates and checkers}\label{cert:chapter}
\label{prov:ch:cert}

The packages of this chapter do not change a rule basis. Each of them fixes a
calculus that is established elsewhere in the article, specifies a format of
proof certificates for it, and gives a program that accepts exactly the
certificates of derivable sequents. The mathematical content is the
specification of the format, the invariants of successful runs, and two kinds
of theorem: a checker is sound and complete for its calculus, or two checkers
of one calculus accept the same certificates. The programs are small, and many
of their steps are safe only because of an invariant of the whole run; these
invariants are stated and proved here. Program text is quoted only where an
invariant refers to it.

All checkers of this chapter share one encoding of syntax
(\cref{cert:sec:codec}) and one instruction format (\cref{cert:sec:base}).
They differ in a small number of independent devices, each of which is
introduced once:

the arithmetic and are not treated in this chapter.

Throughout, a checker reads one finite array of JSON values, except for the
framings of \cref{cert:sec:representation}. Its last element
is the \emph{goal}; the earlier elements are \emph{packets}, consumed from the
end. A run \emph{accepts} if it terminates normally; a failed operation (a
comparison of incomparable values, a missing packet, a wrong number of
fields) rejects. An unused prefix of packets is permitted. Completeness
statements refer to ideal executions without bounds on memory or recursion
depth; a physical run can reject a valid large certificate, and no statement
below equates the resource limits of two programs.

\section{The calculi that are checked}\label{cert:sec:calculi}

This section collects, from \pkg{certified\_zero}, \pkg{kernel\_refinement},
\pkg{compact\_update} and \pkg{validated\_presentation}, the three rule bases
for which the checkers of this chapter are written, and two admissible rules
on which every soundness proof relies.

\begin{definition}[The calculus $\mathcal A$]\label{cert:def:A}
Terms and native formulas are those of \cref{base:sec:terms,base:sec:kernel};
a context is a finite list of formulas. The calculus $\mathcal A$ has the
rules
\begin{gather*}
\infrule{}{\Gamma\proves A}\ (A\in\Gamma)
\qquad
\infrule{}{\Gamma\proves \It{0}{a}{u}{h}=a}
\qquad
\infrule{}{\Gamma\proves \It{\Suc n}{a}{u}{h}=h\subst{\It{n}{a}{u}{h}}{u}}
\\[4pt]
\infrule{\Gamma\proves s=t\quad \Gamma\proves F(s)}{\Gamma\proves F(t)}
\qquad
\infrule{\Gamma\proves \Q{y}B(y)\quad \Gamma\proves B(0)}{\Gamma\proves B(t)}
\qquad
\infrule{\Gamma\proves \D{x}{y}B(x,y)\quad \Gamma,\Q{y}B(b,y)\proves A}{\Gamma\proves A}
\\[4pt]
\infrule{\Gamma,B(t,0)\proves B(t,\Suc z)}{\Gamma\proves \D{x}{y}B(x,y)}
\qquad
\infrule{\Gamma,E(t,z)\proves E(t,\Suc z)}{\Gamma\proves \D{x}{y}E(x,y)}
\end{gather*}
named \rulename{Hyp}, \rulename{I0}, \rulename{IS}, \rulename{Tr},
\rulename{QE}, \rulename{DE}, \rulename{DI}-case and \rulename{DI}-progress.
In \rulename{Tr}, $F(v)$ is an arbitrary native formula template with one
designated numerical variable, which may occur any number of times and below
binders. In \rulename{DE} the parameter $b$ is fresh for $\Gamma$, $A$ and the
parameters of $B$. In both introductions $z$ is fresh for $\Gamma$, $t$ and
the parameters of the matrix, and in \rulename{DI}-progress the matrix $E$ is a
literal equation: its outermost constructor is equality.
\end{definition}

The calculus $\mathcal A$ is the calculus \Lean{IPrf} of the Lean development,
and \Lean{iprf\_iff\_nprf} states that it derives the same sequents as the
native core $\Nat$ of \cref{base:sec:kernel}. Consequently weakening,
renaming of parameters, capture-free numerical substitution and cut of an
assumption are admissible for $\mathcal A$ (\cref{base:sec:structural}), and
$\mathcal A$ inherits the bridge of \cref{base:thm:bridge}. The formula $0=0$
is derivable: with $r\defeq\It{0}{0}{u}{0}$, \rulename{I0} proves $r=0$, and
\rulename{Tr} with $F(v)\equiv(v=0)$ applied to $r=0$ and $F(r)$ gives $0=0$.

\begin{lemma}[Zero-instance supply]\label{cert:lem:supply}
Let $\vec b$ be parameters that do not occur in $\Gamma$ or $A$. If
$\proves H(\vec 0)$ and $\Gamma,H(\vec b)\proves A$, then $\Gamma\proves A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Rename the eigenparameters of the second derivation away from $\vec b$ and
substitute $0$ for every member of $\vec b$. Since $\vec b$ does not occur in
$\Gamma$ or $A$, the result is a derivation of $\Gamma,H(\vec 0)\proves A$.
Cut the assumption $H(\vec 0)$ against its derivation.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Neutral resource]\label{cert:lem:neutral-resource}
If $T$ is a closed theorem, then $\Gamma,T\proves A$ if and only if
$\Gamma\proves A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Weakening gives one direction and cut gives the other. Only renaming of
eigenparameters is used, no numerical substitution.
\end{proof}

\Cref{cert:lem:supply} is the reason why a checker may add a computation
equation to its context without recognizing the equation as an axiom: it is
enough that the instance at $\vec 0$ of the added formula is an instance of
\rulename{I0} or \rulename{IS}. The open formula $H(\vec b)$ need not be
true. For example $H(b)\equiv(\It{0}{0}{v}{0}=b)$ has a computation instance
at $b=0$ and is false at $b=\Suc 0$.

\begin{definition}[Source-selected elimination]\label{cert:def:Aminus}
For a template $F(v)$ and terms $s,t$ put
\[
M(s,t;F)\defeq
\begin{cases}
\Q{v}F(v), & s\text{ is literally }0,\\
s=t, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
The rule \rulename{SE} infers $F(t)$ from $M(s,t;F)$ and $F(s)$. The calculus
$\mathcal A^{-}$ is $\mathcal A$ with \rulename{Tr} and \rulename{QE} replaced
by \rulename{SE}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The guarded-source calculus $\mathcal U$]\label{cert:def:U}
A formula is \emph{public} if it is an equation, or $\D{x}{y}B$ with $B$
public and compound, or $\D{x}{y}E$ with $E$ an equation of the shape
\begin{equation}\label{cert:eq:guard}
\It{y}{0}{u}{h}=\It{y}{0}{u}{k}.
\end{equation}
The calculus $\mathcal U$ has \rulename{Hyp}, \rulename{I0}, \rulename{IS},
\rulename{SE}, \rulename{DE} and one introduction
\[
\infrule{\Gamma,B(t,\eta_B(z))\proves B(t,\Suc z)}{\Gamma\proves\D{x}{y}B(x,y)},
\qquad
\eta_B(z)\defeq\begin{cases}z,&B\text{ a literal equation},\\0,&B\text{ compound},\end{cases}
\]
with $z$ fresh as before. Derivations may use all native formulas, including
$Q$ and unguarded atomic matrices, as auxiliary formulas; a \emph{public
sequent} has public assumptions and a public conclusion. The \emph{auxiliary
reading} $A^{*}$ of a formula in $\Teq$ is the ordinary reading of
\cref{base:sec:reading} except that
$(\D{x}{y}E)^{*}\defeq\exists x\,\forall y\,(E(x,y)\to E(x,\Suc y))$ when $E$ is
a literal equation.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Imported equivalences]\label{cert:rem:imported}
The checkers of \cref{cert:sec:scalar,cert:sec:validated} are written for
$\mathcal A^{-}$ and $\mathcal U$, whose equivalence with $\Nat$ is not a
result of this chapter. The package \pkg{source\_dispatch} proves that
$\mathcal A^{-}$ and $\mathcal A$ derive the same sequents (in Lean:
\Lean{zprf\_iff\_nprf}); every omitted instance of \rulename{Tr}, with source
the literal $0$, is derivable. The packages \pkg{guarded\_core} and
\pkg{guarded\_source} prove that every rule of $\mathcal U$ is valid in $\Teq$
under the auxiliary reading, that $A^{*}$ and the ordinary reading of $A$ are
equivalent in $\Teq$ for public $A$ (the zero instance of a guarded equation
\eqref{cert:eq:guard} is a theorem), and that $\mathcal U$ derives every public
sequent whose ordinary reading is derivable in $\Teq$; the guarded public
language is formalized in Lean in the form \Lean{pgprf\_pub\_iff}. Numerical
substitution into a derivation is not admissible in $\mathcal A^{-}$ or
$\mathcal U$ as a transformation of derivations, because it can turn a source
into the literal $0$. For this reason soundness of a checker of $\mathcal U$
is proved below through the reading in $\Teq$, where
\cref{cert:lem:supply} is available, and not by a direct appeal to
\cref{cert:lem:supply} in $\mathcal U$.
\end{remark}

\section{The sorted arity encoding}\label{cert:sec:codec}

The encoding of this section is used by every compact checker of the chapter;
it is restated, from an earlier checker, by \pkg{certified\_zero},
\pkg{uniform\_premise}, \pkg{prevalidated\_elimination} and
\pkg{monus\_and\_neutral\_major}, whose statements are merged here.

\begin{definition}[Raw trees]\label{cert:def:raw}
A \emph{raw tree} is a scalar or a finite array of raw trees. The
\emph{arity} of an array is its length. A scalar is \emph{numeric} if it is an
integer, a floating-point number or a Boolean; the Booleans are compared and
added as $0$ and $1$. A raw value is \emph{false-valued} if it is numerically
zero, the empty array, or one of the remaining empty JSON values (\texttt{null},
the empty string, the empty object); otherwise it is \emph{true-valued}.
\end{definition}

A raw tree is read at a position of one of two sorts, term or formula, below
some number $p\ge 0$ of \emph{protected slots}. The \emph{depth} of the
position is $3+2p$ at a term position and $2p$ at a formula position, so that
its parity records the sort. Slots are numbered from the innermost, starting
with $0$. A \emph{floor} is an integer $k\le 0$.

\begin{definition}[Sorted reading]\label{cert:def:reading}
Fix a floor $k$. At a term position below $p$ slots a numeric scalar $r$ reads
as
\[
\begin{cases}
0, & 0\le r<3,\\
\text{slot } j, & 3+2j\le r<5+2j\text{ and } j<p,\\
\text{the rigid parameter named } r, & k\le r<0,\\
\text{the hole } w, & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\]
where $w$ is one distinguished variable. Arrays read as follows.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}cll@{}}
\toprule
Arity & At a term position & At a formula position\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0$ & $0=0$\\
$1$ & $\Suc$ of the child & $\D{x}{y}$ of the child\\
$2$ & $0$ & equality of the two children\\
$3$ & $\It{n}{a}{u}{h}$ of the children $n,a,h$ & $\Q{y}$ of the last child\\
$\ge 4$ & $0$ & $0=0$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The child of a unary term array is read below two additional \emph{dummy}
slots, which read as $0$. The children $n$ and $a$ of a ternary term array are
read below one additional dummy slot and the child $h$ below one additional
slot, the state $u$. The children of a binary formula array are read at term
positions below the same slots. The child of a unary formula array is read
below two additional slots, $y$ innermost and $x$ next; the last child of a
ternary formula array is read below one additional slot $y$. A scalar at a
formula position reads as $0=0$. Children that the table does not mention
(those of a binary term array, of an array of arity at least $4$, and the
first two children of a ternary formula array) are \emph{annotations} and
have no reading.
\end{definition}

With child depth $d+5-r$ for a child of an array of arity $r$ at depth $d$,
all the environments of \cref{cert:def:reading} are obtained from one law:
the increments are $4$, $3$ and $2$ at arities $1$, $2$ and $3$, that is, two
slots, a change of sort, and one slot.

\begin{definition}[Canonical encoding]\label{cert:def:enc}
The \emph{canonical encoding} of a term or formula whose free variables are
rigid parameters with negative integer names writes $0$ as the scalar $0$, a
parameter as its name, the slot $j$ as $3+2j$, and
\[
\Suc t\mapsto[t],\qquad \It{n}{a}{u}{h}\mapsto[n,a,h],\qquad
s=t\mapsto[s,t],\qquad \D{x}{y}B\mapsto[B],\qquad \Q{y}B\mapsto[B,B,B],
\]
where the slot numbers of variables are counted through the dummy slots of
\cref{cert:def:reading}. A canonical encoding contains no reference to a
dummy slot, and its reading is the encoded expression.
\end{definition}

The triplication of the matrix of $Q$ serves the export step of the
checker: repeating a unary array $[B]$ three times yields exactly the
canonical closure $[B,B,B]$, and repeating any array of arity at least $2$
yields an array of arity at least $6$, which reads as $0=0$.

\begin{definition}[Opener]\label{cert:def:opener}
For a floor $k$, a raw tree $a$, a raw \emph{image} $t$ or the missing image
$\bot$, and a depth $d$, the opening $O_k(a;t,d)$ is defined by
\[
O_k(a;t,d)\defeq
\begin{cases}
[\,O_k(b;t,d+5-r):b\in a\,], & a\text{ an array of arity }r,\\
a, & a\text{ a scalar with }k\le a<d,\\
O_k(t;\bot,3), & a\text{ another scalar and } d \text{ odd},\\
0, & a\text{ another scalar and } d \text{ even}.
\end{cases}
\]
It is undefined if a comparison is applied to a value that is not numeric, or
to $\bot$. The tree $a$ is \emph{validated at depth $d$ and floor $k$} if
$O_k(a;\bot,d)$ is defined, and \emph{closed at depth $d$ and floor $k$} if
its reading at a position of depth $d$ and floor $k$ contains no hole. The
depth is omitted when it is $0$, the depth of the formula root.
\end{definition}

The image is inserted after being validated at the root term depth $3$ with a
missing image, so an insertion never starts a further substitution; the
opener terminates by structural recursion on finite trees. The checkers
differ in the test that recognizes an array (an explicit type test, the
equation \texttt{a*0 == []}, which separates arrays from the other JSON
values on which it is defined, or an attempted traversal that falls back to
the scalar case), and in the spelling of $\bot$ (\texttt{None}, the complex
number \texttt{0j}, or a function object); each spelling makes every
comparison with $\bot$ fail. The profile that recognizes arrays by attempted
traversal accepts a few further empty iterables as aliases of the empty array
and has one qualification: on a nonempty string an ideal execution does not
terminate, and an actual one rejects through its recursion limit. This
qualification is attached to that profile wherever its size is quoted
(\cref{cert:sec:sizes}).

\begin{lemma}[Semantic opening]\label{cert:lem:opening}
Let $a$ be read at a position of depth $d$ and floor $k$, and suppose that
$a'=O_k(a;t,d)$ is defined.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The tree $a'$ is closed at depth $d$ and floor $k$.
\item If $t=\bot$, then $a$ is closed at depth $d$ and floor $k$, and $a'$ and
$a$ have the same reading.
\item If $t\neq\bot$ and the hole occurs in the reading of $a$, then
$t'=O_k(t;\bot,3)$ is defined, the reading $\tau$ of $t$ at the root term
position is a term whose only free variables are parameters with names in
$[k,0)$, and the reading of $a'$ is the reading of $a$ with $\tau$
substituted, without capture, for the hole. If the hole does not occur, $a'$
and $a$ have the same reading.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Statement (b) is proved first, by induction on $a$. A scalar at a term
position is retained exactly when it lies in $[k,d)$, that is, when it does
not read as the hole; otherwise the comparison with $\bot$ fails. A scalar at
a formula position is retained or replaced by $0$, and both read as $0=0$. At
an array of arity $1$, $2$ or $3$ the children that have a reading are opened
at exactly the depths of their positions, and the induction hypothesis
applies; annotations are traversed, which can make the opening undefined but
does not affect a reading. Arrays of other arities have a constant reading and
keep their arity.

For (c), the same induction applies, with one new case: a scalar that reads as
the hole is replaced by $t'$. By (b) for $t$ at depth $3$, the tree $t'$ reads
as $\tau$ at the root term position, and every scalar of $t'$ at its root
level lies in $[k,3)$, so it reads as $0$ or as a parameter in $[k,0)$; a
scalar of $t'$ below $q$ of its own binders lies in $[k,3+2q)$ and refers to
$0$, to a parameter, or to one of these $q$ binders. Hence $t'$ has the
reading $\tau$ at every term position, below any number of slots: copying it
below further binders captures nothing and moves none of its indices.
Statement (a) follows from (b) and (c), since every retained scalar at a term
position lies in $[k,d)$.
\end{proof}

No claim is made that an opened tree is unchanged by a second opening at
another depth, floor or image: annotations below arrays of arity at least $4$
are traversed at depths that may decrease, and a scalar at a formula position
is not a fixed point of every traversal. At one floor and one depth, without
an image, opening is idempotent (\cref{cert:lem:retraction}). Only readings at
positions that have one are used. Since the
reading is a function of the raw tree, equal raw trees have equal readings.

% ed.: the reports use this fact inside their case analyses ("opens its witness
% slot and retains the index slot") and refer to an inherited decoder proof; it
% is stated and proved here as a separate lemma, with the notion "closed".
\begin{lemma}[Exposure of the outermost slot]\label{cert:lem:exposure}
If $a$ is closed at depth $d+2$ and floor $k$, then its reading at depth
$d$ is its reading at depth $d+2$ with the outermost slot replaced by the hole.
Consequently $O_k(a;t,d)$, when defined, reads as the reading of $a$ at depth
$d+2$ with $\tau$ substituted for the outermost slot.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let the position of depth $d+2$ lie below $p+1$ slots. A scalar $r$ at a term
position with a reading, below $q$ further binders, lies in
$[k,3+2(p+1+q))$ because the reading has no hole. At
depth $d$ the same position has depth $3+2(p+q)$: a scalar in $[k,3+2(p+q))$
keeps its reading, and a scalar in $[3+2(p+q),5+2(p+q))$, which read as slot
$p+q$, the outermost of the $p+1$ slots, now reads as the hole. The second
statement is \cref{cert:lem:opening}(c).
\end{proof}

\Cref{cert:lem:exposure} covers the three openings of a checker. For a
closed goal $[B]$, the matrix $B$ is closed at depth $4$ with slots $y$
and $x$; $O_k(B;t,2)$ instantiates the witness slot $x$ and keeps $y$. For a
matrix $B'$ closed at depth $2$, $O_k(B';t,0)$ instantiates its index slot
$y$. For a closed major $[B]$, the opening $O_k([B,B,B];t,0)$ opens each
copy of $B$ at depth $2$: it instantiates $x$ and leaves $y$ bound by the
closure.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical opening]\label{cert:lem:canonical}
Let $a$ be the canonical encoding of an expression below $p+1$ slots whose
parameters have names in $[k,0)$, read at
depth $d$ below $p$ slots, and let $t$ be the canonical encoding of a term
whose free variables are parameters with names in $[k,0)$. Then $O_k(a;t,d)$
is the canonical encoding of the expression with that term substituted for the
outermost slot, and $O_k(a;\bot,d+2)=a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A canonical encoding uses the scalar $0$, parameter names in $[k,0)$ and slot
scalars $3+2j$, all retained when in range; the exposed slot scalars are
replaced by $O_k(t;\bot,3)=t$. The encoding of a term without free slots does
not depend on the number of slots above it, so the result is canonical.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Freshness]\label{cert:lem:fresh}
If a tree is closed at depth $d$ and floor $k$, its reading contains only
parameters with names in $[k,0)$, and it is closed at depth $d$ with the same
reading at every floor $k'\le k$. Hence the names in $[k',k)$ are fresh for
every formula that is the reading of a tree closed at floor $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Lowering the floor changes the reading only of scalars below $k$, and a tree
closed at floor $k$ has none at a position with a reading, since such a scalar
reads as the hole.
\end{proof}

For canonical data there is exactly one name in $[k-1,k)$, the integer $k-1$.
An arbitrary certificate may contain fractional scalars; then finitely many
names lie in $[k-1,k)$, they are distinct parameters, and the arguments below
treat them as a finite family $\vec b$.

\section{The instruction format and the certified zero}\label{cert:sec:base}

This section follows \pkg{certified\_zero}: the base protocol of all compact
checkers, the restriction of computation that lets the initializer field
select the computation branch, and the correspondence between the checker and
the calculus $\mathcal A$.

\subsection{The base protocol}

\begin{definition}[Base protocol]\label{cert:def:base}
The state of a run consists of a \emph{global floor} $k$, initially $0$, and
the stack of unread packets. The procedure $\mathrm{Check}(a,g)$, for a raw
goal $a$ and a list $g$ of raw trees, performs the following steps.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Compute $a^{\circ}=O_k(a;\bot,0)$. If $a^{\circ}$ is a member of $g$,
return.
\item Remove the last packet. It has exactly four fields $[m,s,u,t]$.
\item If $m$ is true-valued, run $\mathrm{Check}(m,g)$. Afterwards, if
$m=[s,u]$, or if $s$ is false-valued and $m=[t,t,t]$, and if moreover
$O_k(t;u,0)=a$, replace $a$ by $O_k(t;s,0)$.
\item Form the hypothesis $h$.
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
 \item If $m$ is true-valued, $h$ is the threefold repetition of $m$.
 \item If $m$ is false-valued and $u$ is true-valued, then $h=[[s,u,t],u]$
 when $s$ is false-valued, and $h=[[[s],u,t],\,O_k(t;[s,u,t],3)]$ when $s$ is
 true-valued.
 \item If $m$ and $u$ are false-valued, $a$ is an array $[B]$ of arity one;
 replace $a$ by $O_k(B;t,2)$ and put $h=a$.
 \end{enumerate}
\item Decrease $k$ by $1$.
\item Let $\iota=k$ if $m$ is true-valued, or if $m$ is numerically zero and
the arity of $a$ is even; otherwise let $\iota$ be the scalar zero. Run
$\mathrm{Check}\bigl(O_k(a;[k],0),\ g\mathbin{+\!\!+}[O_k(h;\iota,0)]\bigr)$.
\end{enumerate}
The checker reads the goal $a_0$ and runs $\mathrm{Check}(a_0,[\,])$.
\end{definition}

The three branches of step (4) are selected by data: a true-valued major
selects an elimination, a true-valued initializer field $u$ selects a
computation, and otherwise the packet is an introduction. There is no
operation code. A packet is used as follows on canonical encodings; fields
without a role are written $0$, and $[\,]$ stands for any false-valued marker
that is not numerically zero.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Inference & Packet $[m,s,u,t]$ & Effect\\
\midrule
\rulename{Tr} with major $s=u$, template $F$ & $[[s,u],s,u,F]$ & goal $F(u)$ becomes $F(s)$\\
\rulename{QE} with major $\Q{y}B$ & $[[B,B,B],0,u,B]$ & goal $B(u)$ becomes $B(0)$\\
\rulename{DE} with major $\D{x}{y}B$ & $[[B],0,0,0]$ & $\Q{y}B(b,y)$ is added\\
\rulename{I0}, initializer $a$, update $h$ & $[[\,],0,a,h]$ & $\It{0}{a}{v}{h}=a$ is added\\
\rulename{IS}, predecessor $n$ & $[[\,],n,a,h]$ & the \rulename{IS} equation is added\\
\rulename{DI}-case, witness $w$ & $[[\,],0,0,w]$ & goal $\D{x}{y}B$ becomes $B(w,\Suc z)$\\
\rulename{DI}-progress, witness $w$ & $[0,0,0,w]$ & the same, with $E(w,z)$ added\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In a template $F$ the designated variable is encoded as the outermost slot of
a formula read below one slot, so that \cref{cert:lem:exposure} applies; for
\rulename{QE} the template is the matrix itself. A derivation is serialized
with the packets of the major premise after the packet of the inference and
the packets of the continuation after those, and the whole sequence is
reversed, since packets are consumed from the end.

Two points of the definition depend on arguments given below. First, in the
computation branch the field $u$ must be true-valued, so the equations
\rulename{I0} and \rulename{IS} are available only for an initializer that is
not the scalar $0$; \cref{cert:thm:certzero} shows that nothing is lost.
Second, step (6) may open a generated computation equation at the fresh
parameter instead of at zero (this happens when $m$ is numerically zero and
the goal has even arity); \cref{cert:lem:zeroinstance} and
\cref{cert:lem:supply} show that this is sound.

\subsection{Restricted computation and the certified zero}

\begin{definition}\label{cert:def:restricted}
The calculus $\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$ is $\mathcal A$ with its computation
rules restricted by the syntax of their arguments: \rulename{I0} is available
only when the initializer $a$ is not the literal term $0$, and \rulename{IS}
only when neither the predecessor $n$ nor the initializer $a$ is the literal
term $0$. A term that is not the literal $0$ is permitted even if it denotes
zero.
\end{definition}

The restriction on the predecessor is present in earlier checkers of the same
family (\pkg{uniform\_predecessor}); the restriction on the initializer is the
one introduced by \pkg{certified\_zero}. The term
\[
C\defeq\It{\Suc\Suc 0}{\Suc 0}{v}{0}
\]
is a permitted argument, and the instance of \rulename{IS} with predecessor
$\Suc 0$, initializer $\Suc 0$ and update $0$ is permitted and reads $C=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Certified zero]\label{cert:thm:certzero}
Every instance of \rulename{I0} and \rulename{IS} is derivable in
$\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$, by one application of \rulename{Tr} to two permitted
computation instances. Hence $\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$ and $\mathcal A$ derive
the same sequents, over the same terms and formulas.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A permitted instance is immediate. For an excluded instance choose a fresh
variable $w$, with the bound state variable renamed away from $w$, and an
equation $E(w)$ as follows.
\begin{align*}
&\text{\rulename{I0}, initializer }0:
 && E(w)\equiv\bigl(\It{0}{w}{v}{h}=w\bigr);\\
&\text{\rulename{IS}, initializer }0\text{, predecessor }n\neq 0:
 && E(w)\equiv\bigl(\It{\Suc n}{w}{v}{h}=h\subst{\It{n}{w}{v}{h}}{v}\bigr);\\
&\text{\rulename{IS}, predecessor }0\text{, initializer }a\neq 0:
 && E(w)\equiv\bigl(\It{\Suc w}{a}{v}{h}=h\subst{\It{w}{a}{v}{h}}{v}\bigr);\\
&\text{\rulename{IS}, both }0:
 && E(w)\equiv\bigl(\It{\Suc w}{w}{v}{h}=h\subst{\It{w}{w}{v}{h}}{v}\bigr).
\end{align*}
Only the displayed occurrences of the initializer and the predecessor are
replaced by $w$; occurrences of $0$ inside $h$ stay. In each case $E(C)$ is a
permitted computation instance, because $C$ is not the literal $0$, and $E(0)$
is the excluded instance. \rulename{Tr} with the template $E$, applied to the
permitted instance $C=0$ and to $E(C)$, gives $E(0)$. Replacing every excluded
leaf of a derivation in this way gives the inclusion of $\mathcal A$ in
$\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$; the other inclusion is trivial. Contexts and end
sequents are unchanged.
\end{proof}

The construction uses no induction, no earlier computation with initializer
$0$ and no decision about the value of a term. Its cost is explicit: if a
tree derivation has $N$ inference nodes of which $r$ are excluded computation
leaves, the repaired derivation has $N+2r$ nodes; if the node proving $C=0$ is
shared in a directed acyclic representation and $r>0$, it has $N+r+1$ nodes.
These are counts for this construction, not lower bounds.

\subsection{Correspondence with the calculus}

\begin{lemma}[Zero-instance computation]\label{cert:lem:zeroinstance}
In step (4ii) of \cref{cert:def:base}, suppose that the opening
$h'=O_{k-1}(h;\iota,0)$ of step (6) is defined. Let $\vec b$ be the parameters
with names in $[k-1,k)$ that occur in the reading $H(\vec b)$ of $h'$. Then
$H(\vec 0)$ is an instance of \rulename{I0} or \rulename{IS}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The tree $h$ is a binary array, so $h'$ reads as an equation; its left side
is a ternary term array and reads as an iterator with update $\eta$, the
reading of $t$ below the state slot.

Let $s$ be false-valued, so $h=[[s,u,t],u]$. The counter $s$ is traversed by
the opening; a false-valued tree for which this succeeds is a numeric zero or
the empty array and reads as $0$. The initializer $u$ is read on the left
below the dummy slot of the iterator and on the right at the root. The two
readings agree except that a scalar referring to the dummy slot reads as $0$
on the left and as the hole, hence as the reading of $\iota$, on the right.
The reading of $\iota$ is $0$ or the parameter named $k-1$. After the
substitution of $0$ for $\vec b$ both sides have the same initializer
$\alpha$, and $H(\vec 0)$ is $\It{0}{\alpha}{v}{\eta}=\alpha$.

Let $s$ be true-valued, so $h=[[[s],u,t],\,O_k(t;[s,u,t],3)]$. If the reading
of $t$ at depth $3$ contains the hole, that is, if the update uses its state,
then by \cref{cert:lem:opening}(c) the image $[s,u,t]$ is validated at depth
$3$ and floor $k$. This validates $s$ and $u$ below the dummy slot and $t$
below exactly one state slot, so no coordinate of $t$ beyond its state is
left unbound. The right side of $h$ reads as $\eta\subst{\It{\sigma}{\alpha}{v}{\eta}}{v}$,
where $\sigma$ and $\alpha$ are the readings of $s$ and $u$. On the left, $s$
occurs below two further dummy slots of the constructed successor; all its
scalars lie in $[k,5)$, so its reading is again $\sigma$. Thus $h$ is closed
at floor $k$ and reads as the instance of \rulename{IS} with predecessor
$\sigma$. If the update does not use its state, the right side
$O_k(t;[s,u,t],3)$ is $t$ validated at depth $3$, and $\eta$ does not contain
$v$. The left side reads, after the substitution of $0$ for $\vec b$ and for
the hole, as $\It{\Suc\sigma^{0}}{\alpha^{0}}{v}{\eta}$ for some terms
$\sigma^{0},\alpha^{0}$, and $\eta=\eta\subst{\It{\sigma^{0}}{\alpha^{0}}{v}{\eta}}{v}$
because $v$ does not occur in $\eta$. This is again an instance of
\rulename{IS}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness of the base protocol]\label{cert:thm:base-sound}
Let every member of $g$ be closed at floor $k$. If $\mathrm{Check}(a,g)$,
started at floor $k$, returns, then $a$ is closed at floor $k$ and the reading
of $a$ is derivable in $\mathcal A$ from the readings of the members of $g$.
In particular, if the checker accepts a goal $a_0$, the reading of $a_0$ is a
theorem of $\mathcal A$; for a canonical encoding this reading is the encoded
formula.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The proof is by induction on the number of calls in the run. Floors only
decrease, and by \cref{cert:lem:fresh} a tree that is closed at some floor
keeps its reading at all lower floors; readings are written without mention of
the floor. Step (1) validates $a$, so $a$ is closed by
\cref{cert:lem:opening}(b). If the run returns in step (1), $a^{\circ}$ has the
reading of $a$ and is a member of $g$, and \rulename{Hyp} applies.

\emph{Elimination.} Let $m$ be true-valued and let $k'\le k$ be the floor
after step (3). By the induction hypothesis the reading of $m$ is derivable
from $g$, and $m$ is closed at floor $k$. Suppose that the goal is replaced.
If $m=[s,u]$, then $s$ and $u$ are closed at depth $3$ with readings $\sigma$
and $\upsilon$, the major reads $\sigma=\upsilon$, and by
\cref{cert:lem:opening}(c) the old goal $O_{k'}(t;u,0)$ and the new goal
$O_{k'}(t;s,0)$ read as $F(\upsilon)$ and $F(\sigma)$ for the template $F(w)$
that is the reading of $t$ at floor $k'$. By \rulename{Tr} it suffices to
derive $F(\sigma)$. If $s$ is false-valued and $m=[t,t,t]$, then $t$ is closed
at depth $2$, the major reads $\Q{y}B(y)$ for the reading $B$ of $t$ below the
slot $y$, and by \cref{cert:lem:exposure} the old goal reads $B(\upsilon)$. A
false-valued image that is used by an opening is a numeric zero or the empty
array and reads $0$, so the new goal reads $B(0)$, and by \rulename{QE} it
suffices to derive $B(0)$. If the goal is not replaced there is nothing to
show at this point. In each case the current goal $a_1$ is closed at floor
$k'$, and it remains to derive its reading from $g$.

The final call runs at floor $k'-1$ with goal $O_{k'-1}(a_1;[k'-1],0)$, which
has the reading of $a_1$ since $a_1$ contains no hole, and with the additional
hypothesis $h'=O_{k'-1}(h;k'-1,0)$, where $h$ is the threefold repetition of
$m$. All members of the extended context are closed at floor $k'-1$ by
\cref{cert:lem:opening}(a), so the induction hypothesis applies. If $m=[B]$ is
unary, then $h=[B,B,B]$, and by \cref{cert:lem:exposure} $h'$ reads
$\Q{y}B(b,y)$, where $\D{x}{y}B$ is the reading of $m$ and $b$ is the
parameter named $k'-1$. By \cref{cert:lem:fresh}, $b$ is fresh for $g$, for
$a_1$ and for the parameters of $B$, and \rulename{DE} concludes. If $m$ is
an array of arity at least $2$, then $h$ has arity at least $6$ and $h'$ reads
$0=0$; \cref{cert:lem:neutral-resource} concludes. If $m$ is a true-valued
scalar for which the run continues, it is numeric, $h$ is a numeric scalar and
$h'$ reads $0=0$ as well; moreover no replacement of the goal has occurred,
since a scalar is not equal to an array. (The program writes the second
alternative of the test as a value that is the Boolean \texttt{False} when $s$
is true-valued; a true-valued scalar is not equal to it either.)

\emph{Computation.} Let $m$ be false-valued and $u$ true-valued. The goal is
unchanged, and the final call derives its reading from $g$ and the reading
$H(\vec b)$ of $h'$, where $\vec b$ are the parameters with names in
$[k-1,k)$. They are fresh for $g$ and $a$ by \cref{cert:lem:fresh}. By
\cref{cert:lem:zeroinstance} $H(\vec 0)$ is an axiom of $\mathcal A$, and
\cref{cert:lem:supply} concludes.

\emph{Introduction.} Let $m$ and $u$ be false-valued and $a=[B]$. Then $B$ is
closed at depth $4$, and by \cref{cert:lem:exposure} $a_1=O_k(B;t,2)$ reads,
below the slot $y$, as $B(\tau,y)$, where $\D{x}{y}B(x,y)$ is the reading of
$a$ and $\tau$ is the reading of $t$ if the witness slot occurs, and $0$
otherwise; the parameters of $\tau$ have names in $[k,0)$. Let $z$ be the
parameter named $k-1$. In the final call the goal $O_{k-1}(a_1;[k-1],0)$ reads
$B(\tau,\Suc z)$ and the hypothesis $O_{k-1}(a_1;\iota,0)$ reads $B(\tau,z)$
if $\iota=k-1$ and $B(\tau,0)$ if $\iota$ is zero. The parameter $z$ is fresh
for $g$, for $\tau$ and for the parameters of $B$. The opener preserves
arities, so the arity of $a_1$ is the arity of $B$. If it is $2$, $B$ is a
literal equation and \rulename{DI}-progress or \rulename{DI}-case applies. If
it is $1$ or $3$, the arity is odd, $\iota$ is zero, and \rulename{DI}-case
applies to the compound matrix. If it is $0$ or at least $4$, the conclusion
reads $\D{x}{y}(0=0)$, which is derivable by \rulename{DI}-case from the
theorem $0=0$. If $B$ is a scalar, step (6) is undefined and the run rejects.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{cert:rem:base-details}
Three details of the programs lie outside \cref{cert:def:base} and do not
affect \cref{cert:thm:base-sound}. On the empty goal $[\,]$ an introduction
packet is processed with shifted arguments; the reading of that goal is the
theorem $0=0$. In two of the three profiles the replacement of step (3) is
written as a Boolean expression that keeps the old goal when the new one is
false-valued; then the stronger, unchanged obligation is checked. Canonical
formulas are nonempty arrays, so neither detail affects canonical
certificates. Finally a major is always proved before it is used: a mismatch
of the pattern of step (3) leaves the goal unchanged but does not remove the
obligation to prove $m$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Completeness of the base protocol]\label{cert:thm:base-complete}
Let $A$ be a closed formula. If $A$ is derivable in $\mathcal A$, there is a
stack of packets on which the checker accepts the canonical encoding of $A$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
% ed.: the reports refer to an inherited backward serialization of derivations;
% the induction, with its treatment of names and of retained hypotheses, is
% written out here.
By \cref{cert:thm:certzero} there is a derivation in $\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$.
The following statement is proved by induction on the height of derivations:
for every floor $k$, every list $g$ of canonical encodings of formulas with
parameters named in $[k,0)$, and every derivation of $\Gamma\proves A'$ in
$\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$ with $\Gamma$ among the formulas encoded in $g$ and
parameters named in $[k,0)$, there is a stack on which $\mathrm{Check}$, called
at floor $k$ with the canonical encoding of $A'$ and with $g$, returns.
Weakening and renaming of parameters do not increase the height of a
derivation, so the induction hypothesis may be applied to premises in a larger
context and with renamed eigenparameters. By \cref{cert:lem:canonical} the
goal is unchanged by step (1). If it is a member of $g$, the empty stack
suffices; this covers \rulename{Hyp} and also every sequent whose conclusion
happens to be retained already. Otherwise take the last inference of the
derivation and the packet of the table after \cref{cert:def:base}.

For \rulename{Tr}, \rulename{QE} and \rulename{DE} the major is a nonempty
array, and the stack for its derivation, which exists by the induction
hypothesis, is consumed by step (3) and leaves a floor $k'\le k$. By
\cref{cert:lem:canonical} the pattern test of step (3) succeeds for
\rulename{Tr} and \rulename{QE} and replaces the goal by the canonical
encoding of the minor premise; for \rulename{DE} it fails, because a unary
array is equal neither to $[0,0]$ nor to $[0,0,0]$. The final call runs at
floor $k'-1$ with the same goal and a context extended by a formula that
reads $0=0$, or by the canonical encoding of $\Q{y}B(b,y)$ with the
eigenparameter renamed to $k'-1$. The induction hypothesis for the remaining
premise, weakened and renamed, supplies the rest of the stack.

For a computation leaf, which in $\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$ has an initializer
that is not the literal $0$ and, for \rulename{IS}, such a predecessor, the
fields $u$ and, for \rulename{IS}, $s$ are true-valued: the canonical encoding
of a term other than the literal $0$ is a nonempty array or a negative
integer. Step (4ii) forms $[[0,a,h],a]$ or
$[[[n],a,h],O_k(h;[n,a,h],3)]$, which by \cref{cert:lem:canonical} is the
canonical encoding of the computation equation; the canonical encoding of a
term without free slots is the same below the dummy slots. This is the goal,
so the final call returns in its step (1).

For an introduction with witness $w$ the packet has false-valued $m$ and $u$,
step (4iii) opens the matrix at the canonical encoding of $w$, and step (6)
forms the canonical encodings of $B(w,\Suc z)$ and of $B(w,z)$ or $B(w,0)$
with $z$ renamed to $k-1$: the marker $m=0$ selects $B(w,z)$ exactly when the
matrix is an equation, as \rulename{DI}-progress requires, and the marker
$[\,]$ selects $B(w,0)$. The induction hypothesis supplies the stack for the
premise. In all cases the stack consists of the stack of the continuation,
followed by the stack of the major if there is one, followed by the packet of
the last inference. The packet is then consumed first, the major call consumes
exactly its own stack, and the final call consumes the rest; that each call
consumes exactly the stack constructed for it, when this stack is the end of a
longer one, is part of the statement proved by the induction.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Profiles]\label{cert:rem:base-profiles}
\pkg{certified\_zero} delivers three complete programs of the base protocol,
which differ only in the recognition of arrays described after
\cref{cert:def:opener}: an explicit type test, the zero-product test, and
attempted traversal. The first two terminate on every finite input; the third
has the qualification on nonempty strings. All three are complete for
canonical certificates. The package also isolates the effect of the
initializer dispatch from other changes of the source: restoring the earlier
test that the major is the null value, in the three final programs, costs
exactly eight bytes, two tokens and three syntax-tree nodes in each.
\end{remark}

\section{Uniform zero and constructed majors}\label{cert:sec:uzero}

This section follows \pkg{uniform\_zero}. The certified zero of
\cref{cert:thm:certzero} repairs a restriction that exists only because the
scalar $0$ is at once the canonical zero and a false-valued marker. A
different canonical spelling of zero removes the restriction, and the same
observation lets an elimination packet omit its major formula.

\subsection{The recoding}

By \cref{cert:def:reading} the scalars $0$, $1$ and $2$ all read as zero at a
term position. Let $R$ map a canonical encoding to the tree obtained by
replacing each scalar $0$ by the scalar $1$; arrays, slot scalars and
parameter names are unchanged. The trees $R(A)$ are the \emph{canonical
encodings with uniform zero}: the term $0$ is the scalar $1$, the term
$\Suc 0$ is $[1]$. The recoding applies to expression fields of packets only;
the markers $0$ and $[\,]$ in the major field keep their role.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform truth value]\label{cert:lem:truthy}
Every canonical encoding with uniform zero of a term is true-valued, every
such encoding of a formula is a nonempty array, and $R(A)$ has the same
reading as $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The scalar $1$ is true-valued, a parameter name is negative, a slot scalar is
at least $3$, and the encodings of $\Suc$, of the iterator, of equations, of
$D$ and of $Q$ are nonempty arrays. The scalars $0$ and $1$ have the
same reading at every term position, and $R$ changes nothing else.
\end{proof}

Truth value is a property of the syntax: a true-valued term may denote zero.

Let $O^{+}_k$ be the opener of \cref{cert:def:opener} with one change: a scalar
$a$ with $k\le a<d$ is returned as $a$ if it is true-valued and as the scalar
$1$ otherwise.

\begin{lemma}[Opening commutes with the recoding]\label{cert:lem:recoding}
For canonical encodings $A$ and $t$ as in \cref{cert:lem:canonical},
\[
O^{+}_k\bigl(R(A);R(t),d\bigr)=R\bigl(O_k(A;t,d)\bigr),
\]
and likewise with the missing image when no image is used. Opening a
canonical template with uniform zero at the raw image $0$ gives the same
result as opening it at the raw image $1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
First let no image be used; the proof is by induction on $A$. A scalar $0$ of
$A$ occurs at a term position, where it is retained by $O_k$; in $R(A)$ it is
the scalar $1$, retained by $O^{+}_k$. Every other retained scalar is
true-valued and unchanged on both sides. Arrays are traversed at the same
depths on both sides. A canonical formula has no scalar at a formula position,
so the formula-position clause does not arise. With an image, the only new
case is an exposed scalar, replaced on the left by $O^{+}_k(R(t);\bot,3)$ and
on the right by $R(O_k(t;\bot,3))$, which are equal by the first part. For the
last statement, $O^{+}_k(0;\bot,3)=1=O^{+}_k(1;\bot,3)$ at every floor.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Semantic opening for $O^{+}$]\label{cert:lem:plus-opening}
\Cref{cert:lem:opening,cert:lem:exposure,cert:lem:fresh} hold for $O^{+}_k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The only changed result is a false-valued numeric scalar retained by the range
test (the integer or floating-point zero, or the Boolean \texttt{False}),
which is returned as $1$. At a term position both read as $0$; at a formula
position both read as $0=0$. All other clauses are those of $O_k$.
\end{proof}

The identity of \cref{cert:lem:recoding} is restricted to canonical encodings;
for arbitrary raw trees only the statement about readings in
\cref{cert:lem:plus-opening} is available, and only it is used for soundness.
In contrast with a definitional reduction of $\It{0}{a}{v}{h}$ to $a$, the
recoding deletes no node and no slot, so no index is shifted.

\subsection{Constructed majors and local floors}

\begin{definition}[Uniform-zero protocols]\label{cert:def:uzero}
The \emph{uniform-zero protocol} is the base protocol with $O^{+}$ in place of
$O$ and with \emph{local floors}: the floor is an argument
$\mathrm{Check}(a,g,k)$ of the procedure, the call for the major in step (3)
receives the caller's floor $k$ and leaves it unchanged, and step (5)
decreases the caller's own floor. The \emph{constructed-major protocol}
changes, in addition, step (3): if the field $m$ is true-valued, the major is
\[
M(m,s,u,t)\defeq
\begin{cases}
[s,u], & s\text{ true-valued},\\
[t,t,t], & s\text{ false-valued and }t\text{ true-valued},\\
m, & s,t\text{ false-valued};
\end{cases}
\]
the checker runs $\mathrm{Check}(M,g,k)$, and afterwards, if
$O^{+}_k(t;u,0)=a$ and $O^{+}_k(t;s,0)$ is true-valued, replaces $a$ by
$O^{+}_k(t;s,0)$. Steps (4)--(6) use $M$ in place of $m$.
\end{definition}

Local floors are taken over from the package \pkg{local\_scopes}. They are
justified by the same induction as the global floor.

\begin{lemma}[Local floors]\label{cert:lem:local}
\Cref{cert:thm:base-sound,cert:thm:base-complete} hold for the base protocol
with local floors.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In the proof of \cref{cert:thm:base-sound} the induction hypothesis for the
major call is the statement that the reading of $m$ is derivable from $g$; it
does not mention the names that the call allocates, which are eigenparameters
of that derivation. With local floors $k'=k$ in the elimination case, the
templates are opened at the caller's floor, and the final call at floor $k-1$
has an eigenparameter that is fresh for $g$ and for the goal, both closed at
floor $k$. The rest of the proof is unchanged. For completeness the stack of
the major derivation is produced at floor $k$ and that of the continuation at
floor $k-1$; names used inside the major derivation are reused, which is a
renaming of eigenparameters in disjoint subderivations. No call changes the
context of its caller, since a context is extended by forming a new list.
\end{proof}

With local floors and an initial context of $c$ entries, every call satisfies
$k=c-\lvert g\rvert$: a major call keeps both, and a continuation appends one
entry and decreases $k$. The floor is nevertheless not a function of the
context that the program could recompute at entry for free; see
\cref{cert:rem:floor-shortcut}.

\begin{proposition}[Soundness of constructed majors]\label{cert:prop:constructed}
\Cref{cert:thm:base-sound} holds for the constructed-major protocol.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cert:lem:plus-opening,cert:lem:local} only step (3) needs an
argument. If $s$ is true-valued, the proved major is $[s,u]$ and reads
$\sigma=\upsilon$; a successful comparison identifies the goal with
$F(\upsilon)$ for the reading $F$ of the template $t$, the new goal is
$F(\sigma)$, and \rulename{Tr} applies. No separately supplied formula is
claimed to have these endpoints. If $s$ is false-valued and $t$ true-valued,
the proved major is $[t,t,t]$ and reads $\Q{y}F(y)$; a false-valued image that
is used reads $0$, so the new goal is $F(0)$ and \rulename{QE} applies. If $s$
and $t$ are false-valued, the supplied $m$ is proved and exported as in the
base protocol; the comparison $O^{+}_k(t;u,0)=a$ can then succeed only for a
template $t$ that is a false-valued scalar or the empty array, in which case
$O^{+}_k(t;s,0)$ is false-valued and the goal is not replaced.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Direct canonical compilation]\label{cert:thm:uzero-complete}
Every derivation in $\mathcal A$, with unrestricted \rulename{I0} and
\rulename{IS}, of a closed formula has a certificate in the constructed-major
protocol on canonical encodings with uniform zero. No computation leaf is
replaced, and the compilation is the identity on readings.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The induction of \cref{cert:thm:base-complete} is repeated with
\cref{cert:lem:recoding} in place of \cref{cert:lem:canonical} and with the
packets
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Inference & Packet $[m,s,u,t]$\\
\midrule
\rulename{Tr} through $F$, major $s=u$ & $[1,R(s),R(u),R(F)]$\\
\rulename{QE} from $\Q{y}B$, target $u$ & $[1,0,R(u),R(B)]$\\
\rulename{DE} from $\D{x}{y}B$ & $[R(\D{x}{y}B),0,0,0]$\\
\rulename{I0}, initializer $a$, update $h$ & $[0,0,R(a),R(h)]$\\
\rulename{IS}, predecessor $n$ & $[0,R(n),R(a),R(h)]$\\
\rulename{DI}-case, witness $w$ & $[[\,],0,0,R(w)]$\\
\rulename{DI}-progress, witness $w$ & $[0,0,0,R(w)]$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
By \cref{cert:lem:truthy} the source $R(s)$ of a transport is true-valued even
when $s$ is the term $0$, and a matrix $R(B)$ is a nonempty array, so the
constructed major is exactly the equation or the closure of the inference.
The initializer $R(a)$ is true-valued for every term $a$, so every computation
leaf selects the computation branch. In \rulename{I0} the generated counter is
the raw marker $0$, which the opening of the hypothesis returns as the
canonical $1$; in \rulename{IS} the predecessor $R(n)$ is true-valued even for
$n=0$, and the generated counter $[1]$ is the canonical successor of zero. The
zero images of step (6) become the canonical $1$ as well. The recoding does
not change the root of a matrix, so the restriction of progress to literal
equations is unchanged. The arrays of arity $6$ or $9$ that equality and
closure majors add to the context are not used by the certificate.
\end{proof}

By \cref{cert:lem:truthy} the consequence relation presented on canonical
encodings is that of $\mathcal A$, read through the identity, so the bridge
of \cref{base:thm:bridge} applies without change.

\subsection{Sizes of certificates}

Let $\lvert X\rvert$ be the length in bytes of the JSON text of $X$ without
whitespace. Then $\lvert[X,Y]\rvert=\lvert X\rvert+\lvert Y\rvert+3$ and
$\lvert[B,B,B]\rvert=3\lvert B\rvert+4$. In a transport packet the major field
$[s,u]$ is replaced by the marker $1$ while $s$ and $u$ remain in their own
fields, which saves exactly $\lvert s\rvert+\lvert u\rvert+2$ bytes; in a
closure packet the field $[B,B,B]$ is replaced by $1$, which saves exactly
$3\lvert B\rvert+3$ bytes. No inference and no premise is removed by these
two changes, and for one derivation with one allocation of names the savings
add over its packets, since the recoding of zero replaces one digit by
another.

Separately, the direct serialization of computation leaves removes the
repairs of \cref{cert:thm:certzero}: a derivation with $N$ nodes and $r$
excluded leaves has $N$ packets instead of $N+2r\le 3N$, when neither
serialization closes an additional goal by a retained hypothesis. The
constructed major copies fields that are present at most three times and $C$
is a fixed term, so both translations are linear in the encoded proof; the
triplication of matrices in the encoding of $Q$, which can be exponential
relative to a shared representation, is not removed.

\begin{example}
The equation $\It{0}{0}{v}{0}=0$ has, in the base protocol with the repair of
\cref{cert:thm:certzero}, a certificate of three packets and $97$ bytes. With
uniform zero the certificate is
\[
\texttt{[[0,0,1,1],[[1,1,1],1]]},
\]
one packet and $23$ bytes: the goal $[[1,1,1],1]$ and the packet of
\rulename{I0} with initializer and update $1$. The package reports, for sixteen
selected examples, $120$ packets and $5029$ bytes in the base protocol, $90$
packets and $3801$ bytes with uniform zero, and $90$ packets and $2955$ bytes
with constructed majors. These are measurements of particular certificates,
not minimal sizes.
\end{example}

The gain is in the certificates. Under a comparison with a matched control
that already uses local floors and the shared hypothesis expression of
\cref{cert:sec:neutral}, uniform zero costs five bytes of checker source and
constructed majors a further six (\cref{cert:sec:sizes}).

\section{Neutral major premises}\label{cert:sec:neutral}

This section merges \pkg{uniform\_premise} and the certificate protocol of
\pkg{monus\_and\_neutral\_major}, which are two write-ups of one device, and
adds the composition with local floors of \pkg{neutral\_scope}. In the base
protocol a packet has a major premise only if its first field is true-valued.
Here every packet has one: a computation or an introduction names a fixed
proved truth, and the test for the presence of a major disappears. The safety
of the resulting program rests on an invariant of successful runs.

\subsection{The protocol and its hazard}

\begin{definition}[Neutral-major protocol]\label{cert:def:neutral}
The neutral-major protocol is the base protocol of \cref{cert:def:base} with
the following changes.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(N\arabic*)]
\item The initial call is $\mathrm{Check}(a_0,[[\,]])$: the context contains
the empty array, which reads $0=0$.
\item In step (3), $\mathrm{Check}(m,g)$ is run for every packet, whether or
not $m$ is true-valued. The replacement test that follows is unchanged.
\item In step (4), branch (i) is taken if the threefold repetition of $m$ is
true-valued, branch (ii) if it is not and $u$ is true-valued, and branch
(iii) otherwise.
\item In step (6), $\iota=k$ if $m$ is true-valued, or if the arity of $a$
taken modulo $2$ is numerically equal to $u$; otherwise $\iota$ is zero.
\end{enumerate}
On canonical certificates a packet without a substantive major has $m=[\,]$,
an introduction by cases has $u=[\,]$, and an introduction by progress has
$u=0$.
\end{definition}

The call $\mathrm{Check}([\,],g)$ returns in its step (1) without reading a
packet and without changing the floor, because $[\,]$ is a member of every
context of the run. The mode of an introduction moves from the first field to
the initializer field, which is false-valued in an introduction.

The program evaluates the test of step (3) as membership of $m$ in the pair
consisting of $[s,u]$ and of the value that is $[t,t,t]$ if $s$ is
false-valued and the Boolean \texttt{False} otherwise. In the base protocol
the test is reached only for a true-valued $m$. Now it is reached for every
$m$, and the Boolean \texttt{False} is equal to the numeric scalar $0$. If a
numeric zero could be a proved major, a packet with $m=0$ and a true-valued
$s$ would replace the goal $F(u)$ by $F(s)$ with no equation proved. The
protocol is therefore sound only if a numeric scalar is never a major of a
returning call, and this is what the invariant below establishes. It holds
because the initial context is $[[\,]]$ and not $[0]$.

\subsection{The invariant of successful runs}

\begin{lemma}[Root openings]\label{cert:lem:root}
For a numeric scalar $r$ and any image $t$,
\[
O_k(r;t,0)=\begin{cases}r,&k\le r<0,\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
\]
which is numeric and does not depend on $t$. If $O_k(a;t,0)$ is defined and
$a$ is an array, the result is an array; if the result is numeric, $a$ is
numeric.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The depth $0$ is even, so a scalar outside $[k,0)$ is replaced by $0$ and no
image is consulted. An array is mapped to an array by the first clause of
\cref{cert:def:opener}, and a scalar that is not numeric fails the comparison.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[No numeric goal]\label{cert:lem:nonumeric}
In the neutral-major protocol there is no floor, no stack and no list $g$ of
arrays for which $\mathrm{Check}(a,g)$ returns with a numeric goal $a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose there is such a run, and choose one with the least number of calls,
over all floors, stacks and contexts. By \cref{cert:lem:root} the tree
$a^{\circ}$ is numeric, and no numeric scalar is equal to an array, so the run
does not return in step (1). It reads a packet, and $\mathrm{Check}(m,g)$
returns. If $m$ were numeric, this call would be a smaller run of the excluded
kind; so $m$ is not numeric, and since its opening is defined, $m$ is an
array.

If the replacement test of step (3) succeeds, then $O_k(t;u,0)=a$ is numeric,
so $t$ is numeric by \cref{cert:lem:root}, and $O_k(t;s,0)=O_k(t;u,0)$ by the
same lemma. The goal is numeric after step (3) in every case.

If $m$ is nonempty, $h$ is its threefold repetition, an array. If $m$ is empty
and $u$ is true-valued, $h$ is a binary array. In the remaining case step
(4iii) requires the goal to be an array of arity one, which a numeric scalar
is not, and the run rejects. So $h$ is an array, its opening in step (6) is an
array, and the final call has a numeric goal $O_k(a;[k],0)$ and a context of
arrays. It is a smaller run of the excluded kind, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Contexts of arrays]\label{cert:lem:arrays}
In a run of the neutral-major protocol from $\mathrm{Check}(a_0,[[\,]])$,
every call has a context all of whose members are arrays, and every major $m$
whose call returns is an array.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The initial context consists of the array $[\,]$. Suppose a call with a
context of arrays reaches step (3). The major call receives the same context.
If it returns, $m$ is an array by \cref{cert:lem:nonumeric}. The hypothesis
$h$ is then the threefold repetition of a nonempty array, or a binary array,
or, in branch (iii), the opened matrix $a=O_k(B;t,2)$. In the last case $m$
is the empty array, so step (6) evaluates the arity of $a$, which is undefined
for a scalar; if the final call is reached, $h=a$ is an array. The opening of
an array is an array, and the new context is formed by appending it to a copy
of the old one, so no call changes the context of another.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The unguarded test]\label{cert:cor:matcher}
At every point of a run that is reached after the return of a major call, the
test of step (3) succeeds if and only if $m$ is a nonempty array and either
$m=[s,u]$, or $s$ is false-valued and $m=[t,t,t]$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cert:lem:arrays} $m$ is an array. An array is not equal to the
Boolean \texttt{False}, and the empty array is equal neither to $[s,u]$ nor to
$[t,t,t]$.
\end{proof}

The corollary states that, on the states that can occur, the test has the
value of the same test guarded by the condition that $m$ is true-valued,
although the two differ on arbitrary values. Two mutations show that the
hypotheses matter. If the initial context is $[0]$, the numeric zero is a
proved major, and a certificate of $\Suc 0=0$ is accepted. If the comparison
in (N4) uses the field $s$ in place of $u$, an introduction with $s=1$ obtains
the hypothesis $B(t,z)$ for a matrix of odd arity, that is, progress for a
compound matrix; the field $s$ is unrestricted in an introduction, whereas
$u$ is false-valued there and no false-valued value is numerically equal to
$1$. Both mutants are constructed and observed by the packages.

\subsection{Correspondence}

\begin{theorem}[Neutral-major protocol]\label{cert:thm:neutral}
If the checker of the neutral-major protocol accepts a goal, the reading of
the goal is a theorem of $\mathcal A$. If a closed formula is a theorem of
$\mathcal A$, its canonical encoding is accepted on some stack.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Soundness.} The statement of \cref{cert:thm:base-sound} is proved for
contexts whose members are arrays closed at the current floor, by the same
induction. By \cref{cert:cor:matcher} the elimination case applies verbatim
when $m$ is a nonempty array. If $m$ is the empty array, its reading is the
theorem $0=0$, the goal is not replaced, and the packet is a computation or
an introduction. In an introduction $u$ is false-valued, and by (N4)
$\iota=k$ exactly when the arity of the opened matrix is even and $u$ is
numerically zero; so the hypothesis $B(\tau,z)$ is granted only to a matrix of
even arity, and the case analysis on the arity in the proof of
\cref{cert:thm:base-sound} applies. In a computation $u$ is true-valued, and
(N4) can give $\iota=k$ when $u$ is numerically $1$; \cref{cert:lem:zeroinstance}
holds for either value of $\iota$, so \cref{cert:lem:supply} applies as
before. The result is a derivation of the reading of the goal from $0=0$, and
\cref{cert:lem:neutral-resource} removes this assumption.

\emph{Completeness.} By \cref{cert:thm:certzero} take a derivation in
$\mathcal A_{\mathrm r}$ and repeat the induction of
\cref{cert:thm:base-complete} with contexts that contain, in addition, the
empty array. The packets are those of the base protocol with $[\,]$ in the
major field of computations and introductions and with the mode in the field
$u$. A canonical goal is a nonempty array and is never equal to $[\,]$, so
the additional member of the context closes no goal of the derivation; the
major call of a packet without substantive major returns at once and consumes
nothing.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A second sufficient argument]\label{cert:rem:bottom}
One may read every numeric scalar at a formula position as the contradiction
$0=\Suc 0$ instead of $0=0$. \Cref{cert:lem:opening} remains true, since such
a scalar is retained or replaced by the scalar $0$. Under this reading a
returning call with a numeric major would prove the contradiction, and the
conclusion of its parent would follow by explosion whatever the test of step
(3) does. This gives soundness without \cref{cert:lem:nonumeric}. The
invariant is the stronger statement: numeric majors do not occur, so the
reading of scalars need not be changed.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Sources]\label{cert:rem:neutral-sources}
\pkg{uniform\_premise} proves \cref{cert:lem:nonumeric,cert:lem:arrays} as one
simultaneous induction on successful runs (every goal and every appended
hypothesis is an array), for the profiles with an exact test for arrays, and
states \cref{cert:rem:bottom}. \pkg{monus\_and\_neutral\_major} proves them by
the minimal counterexample used above and treats, in addition, the profile
with attempted traversal: there the empty string and the empty object open to
the empty array, an object whose only key is the empty string opens to
$[[\,]]$, a true-valued object cannot be a major because its threefold
repetition is undefined, and a nonempty string has no finite successful
opening. The statements above then hold with ``array'' read as ``array or one
of these empty aliases''. The two packages report different programs
(\cref{cert:sec:sizes}); their protocols coincide.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Composition with local floors}

\begin{definition}\label{cert:def:neutral-local}
The \emph{local neutral-major protocol} is the neutral-major protocol with
local floors (\cref{cert:def:uzero}).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Composed checker]\label{cert:thm:neutral-local}
\Cref{cert:lem:nonumeric,cert:lem:arrays,cert:cor:matcher} and
\cref{cert:thm:neutral} hold for the local neutral-major protocol.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The proofs of the three invariants do not refer to the floor except through
\cref{cert:lem:root}, which holds at every floor; the minimal counterexample
is chosen over all floors. The proof of \cref{cert:thm:neutral} combines with
\cref{cert:lem:local}. The context of a caller is not changed by a major
call, because every extension forms a new list.
\end{proof}

The programs of \pkg{neutral\_scope} write step (4), as modified by (N3), as
one Boolean chain: the threefold repetition of $m$, or else the computation
equation if $u$ is true-valued, or else the introduction. By
\cref{cert:lem:arrays} the threefold repetition is true-valued exactly for a
nonempty array $m$, and the computation equation is a binary array, hence
true-valued; so the chain takes the branch that (N3) prescribes, and the
introduction is reached exactly when $m$ and $u$ are false-valued. The
missing image is spelled \texttt{0j}, a value that the JSON reader cannot
produce and that fails every comparison. A further profile of the package
replaces the final call by a loop over an immutable context; this
transformation is treated once, in \cref{cert:thm:loop}. The package constructs
two mutants: the one with initial context $[0]$ described above, and one in
which the loop extends a shared mutable list, which accepts a certificate of
$0=\Suc 0$.

\section{Fixing an elimination before its major is proved}\label{cert:sec:order}

This section follows \pkg{prevalidated\_elimination} and the checker part of
\pkg{scope\_and\_closure}. The order of two steps of the base protocol is
exchanged: the template of an elimination is opened, and the goal replaced,
before the major premise is proved. The proof of the major can then be folded
into the expression that forms the hypothesis.

\subsection{The global-floor version}

\begin{definition}[Prevalidated protocol]\label{cert:def:preval}
The prevalidated protocol is the base protocol of \cref{cert:def:base} with
step (3) replaced by the following two steps.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[($3'$)] If $m=[s,u]$, or if $s$ is false-valued and $m=[t,t,t]$, and if
moreover $O_k(t;u,0)=a$ and $O_k(t;s,0)$ is true-valued, replace $a$ by
$O_k(t;s,0)$.
\item[($3''$)] If $m$ is true-valued, run $\mathrm{Check}(m,g)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

The program evaluates the test of ($3'$) as membership of $m$ in the pair
consisting of $[s,u]$ and of the value that is $[t,t,t]$ if $s$ is
false-valued and the complex number \texttt{1j} otherwise, without a test
that $m$ is true-valued; and it forms the hypothesis of branch (4i) as
\texttt{c(m,g) or m*3}. One of the three delivered profiles replaces the goal
also when $O_k(t;s,0)$ is false-valued; \cref{cert:lem:frozen} covers both
forms, and canonical minor premises are nonempty arrays.

\begin{lemma}[Two properties of the program text]\label{cert:lem:preval-text}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item No value produced by the JSON reader is equal to \texttt{1j}. Hence the
membership test succeeds exactly under the condition of ($3'$), and then $m$
is a nonempty array.
\item Every returning call of the checking procedure returns the null value.
Hence \texttt{c(m,g) or m*3} proves the major and then has the value of the
threefold repetition of $m$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) The values of the reader are arrays, strings, objects, the null value,
Booleans and real numbers, including its nonfinite numbers; none is equal to
a complex number with nonzero imaginary part. If the test succeeds, $m$ is
$[s,u]$ or $[t,t,t]$, a nonempty array. (b) A call returns in step (1) with
no value or after its final call with no value; this is an induction on the
run. A failure inside the major call propagates and the hypothesis is not
formed.
\end{proof}

Writing the Boolean \texttt{False} for the excluded alternative in (a) is
unsound here: it is equal to the numeric $0$, the marker of progress, so a
packet with $m=0$ and a true-valued $s$ would pass the test and, having a
false-valued major, would prove nothing. The package exhibits a false closed
equation accepted after this replacement. Likewise, spelling the missing
image as the integer $0$ instead of a value that fails comparisons fills
unbound holes and accepts a goal that contains an unallocated name.

\begin{lemma}[Frozen elimination]\label{cert:lem:frozen}
In step ($3'$), let the goal $a$ and the context be closed at floor $k$, and
suppose that the goal is replaced by $a_1=O_k(t;s,0)$. If the major call of
step ($3''$) returns, leaving a floor $k'\le k$, then $a_1$ is closed at floor
$k'$ with the same reading as at floor $k$, and the reading of $a$ is
derivable in $\mathcal A$ from the readings of $m$ and $a_1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cert:lem:opening}(a) $a_1$ is closed at floor $k$: every name in it
was admitted at floor $k$, and so were the names of the inserted image. By
\cref{cert:lem:fresh} its reading is the same at floor $k'$. The major call
returns, and its step (1) validates $m$ at floor $k$, so $m$ is closed at
floor $k$. If
$m=[s,u]$, the two openings of $t$ at the one floor $k$ read $F(\upsilon)$ and
$F(\sigma)$ for one template $F$, and the reading of $m$ is $\sigma=\upsilon$;
\rulename{Tr} applies. If $s$ is false-valued and $m=[t,t,t]$, the openings
read $B(\upsilon)$ and $B(0)$ by \cref{cert:lem:exposure}, the reading of $m$
is $\Q{y}B(y)$, and \rulename{QE} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Prevalidated protocol]\label{cert:thm:preval}
The checker of the prevalidated protocol is sound and complete for
$\mathcal A$ in the sense of \cref{cert:thm:base-sound,cert:thm:base-complete}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For soundness, the induction of \cref{cert:thm:base-sound} is repeated; its
elimination case uses \cref{cert:lem:frozen} in place of the argument about
openings at the floor $k'$, and the major is proved in step ($3''$) by the
induction hypothesis, by \cref{cert:lem:preval-text}. A replacement in step
($3'$) is followed by step ($3''$), since $m$ is then true-valued; if the major
call fails, the run rejects.

For completeness, the stacks of \cref{cert:thm:base-complete} are accepted
without change. In a canonical packet the designated variable of a template
is an exposed slot scalar, which is a hole at every floor, and all other
names are admitted at floor $k$; so the openings of step ($3'$) at floor $k$
are the trees that the base protocol computes at floor $k'$. Canonical minor
premises are nonempty arrays, so the condition that $O_k(t;s,0)$ is
true-valued removes no canonical inference.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Raw acceptance is not preserved]\label{cert:prop:incomparable}
There is a stack that the prevalidated checker accepts and the base checker
rejects.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $o=[0]$, $r=[0,o,0]$, $M=[r,o]$ and $c=[0,0,o,0]$, and consider the stack
$[\,c,\ c,\ [M,r,o,[-1,o]],\ [o,o]\,]$ with goal $[o,o]$, which reads
$\Suc 0=\Suc 0$. The term $r$ reads $\It{0}{\Suc 0}{v}{0}$, and the packet $c$
supplies the equation $r=\Suc 0$. In the prevalidated order the template
$[-1,o]$ is opened at floor $0$, where the scalar $-1$ is a hole: the opening
at $u=o$ is the goal, and the goal becomes $[r,o]$. The major $M=[r,o]$ is
then proved by one packet $c$, and the continuation proves the new goal
$[r,o]$ by the other. In the base order the major is proved first, which
lowers the floor to $-1$; the scalar $-1$ of the template is then an admitted
name, the opening at $u$ is $[-1,o]$, the test fails, the goal remains
$[o,o]$, and the remaining packet does not prove it.
\end{proof}

The package states that the two sets of accepted stacks are incomparable and
supplies a stack accepted only in the base order; that stack is not
reproduced in its report (\cref{cert:sec:questions}). The two checkers agree
on canonical certificates and present the same consequence relation, by
\cref{cert:thm:preval}.

\subsection{The local-floor version}

With local floors the exchange is an identity of accepted inputs. This is the
checker result of \pkg{scope\_and\_closure}, which applies it to the local
neutral-major programs of \cref{cert:def:neutral-local} and to a loop profile
of the same protocol.

\begin{theorem}[Equality of accepted certificates]\label{cert:thm:reorder}
Consider a checker with local floors in which the major call is a separate
statement before the replacement test, and the checker obtained from it by
evaluating the replacement test first and placing the major call at the head
of the Boolean chain that forms the hypothesis. The two checkers accept the
same finite inputs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The replacement test reads the packet, the current goal and the opener at the
caller's floor. It reads no packet from the stack and changes neither the
context nor the floor; its only effect is the replacement of the caller's
goal. The major call receives the same arguments $m$, $g$ and $k$ in both
programs, has its own floor and its own binding of the context, does not
refer to the caller's goal, and extends contexts only by forming new lists or
tuples. Its one shared effect is the consumption of packets, which the
replacement test does not touch. Hence the two operations commute on every
state. By \cref{cert:lem:preval-text}(b) the major call contributes a
false value to the chain, so the hypothesis is formed from the same remaining
operands. An induction on the returning run, applied to the major calls and
the continuations, shows that each program returns exactly when the other
does, with the same goal, floor and remaining stack. A failure of either
operation prevents acceptance in both orders; only the point of rejection can
differ.
\end{proof}

\Cref{cert:prop:incomparable} does not contradict \cref{cert:thm:reorder}: with
a global floor the major call lowers the floor at which the test is evaluated,
and with local floors it does not. The decrement of step (5) is not moved in
either version; the witness of an introduction is opened before the fresh
name is allocated.

\section{Scalar neutral premises and loops}\label{cert:sec:scalar}

This section covers the checker parts of \pkg{kernel\_refinement} and
\pkg{compact\_update}, the compact profiles of \pkg{validated\_core}, and the
compact changes of \pkg{checked\_refinement} and
\pkg{deterministic\_introduction}. The programs are checkers of the
guarded-source calculus $\mathcal U$ of \cref{cert:def:U}. Their protocol is
taken from \pkg{guarded\_source}; the scalar spelling of the neutral premise
is taken from \pkg{reversal\_core}. \pkg{kernel\_refinement} and
\pkg{compact\_update} apply that spelling to the same programs independently
and report the same sizes; both describe the result as a composition.

\subsection{The guarded-source protocol}

\begin{definition}[Guarded-source protocol]\label{cert:def:gs}
The guarded-source protocol has local floors and the following steps for
$\mathrm{Check}(a,g,k)$; steps (1), (2) and (5) are those of
\cref{cert:def:base}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(3)] Let $M^{\circ}$ be $[s,u]$ if $s$ is true-valued and $[t,t,t]$
otherwise. If $m=M^{\circ}$, $O_k(t;u,0)=a$ and $O_k(t;s,0)$ is true-valued,
replace $a$ by $O_k(t;s,0)$.
\item[(4)] Run $\mathrm{Check}(m,g,k)$. Form $h$ as in (N3) of
\cref{cert:def:neutral}.
\item[(6)] Let $\iota=k$ if $m$ is true-valued or the arity of $a$ is $2$,
and zero otherwise. Run the final call as in \cref{cert:def:base}.
\end{enumerate}
The initial context is $[0]$, and a packet without substantive major has
$m=0$. The \emph{native protocol} is the same with (N4) of
\cref{cert:def:neutral} in place of step (6).
\end{definition}

Step (3) implements \rulename{SE}: the source field selects the shape of the
major, an equation for a true-valued source and a closure for a false-valued
one. Step (6) implements the single introduction of $\mathcal U$: its mode is
read off the root of the matrix, and a packet carries no mode. The native
protocol keeps the free choice of mode and is a checker of $\mathcal A^{-}$;
it is the proof kernel of the validated checkers of \cref{cert:sec:validated}.

\begin{proposition}[Scalar neutral premise]\label{cert:prop:scalar}
In the guarded-source protocol let every member of $g$ be closed at floor
$k$. If $\mathrm{Check}(a,g,k)$ returns, then $a$ is closed at floor $k$ and
the auxiliary reading of $a$ is derivable in $\Teq$ from the auxiliary
readings of the members of $g$. Conversely, every closed theorem of
$\mathcal U$ has a certificate on which its canonical encoding is accepted.
The same holds for the native protocol with the ordinary reading and
$\mathcal A^{-}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
% ed.: the reports argue soundness in two sentences ("each retained item is a
% decoded assumption, a computation theorem, or administrative truth"); the
% induction through the auxiliary reading is written out here, and it uses that
% the candidate of step (3) is always an array.
\emph{Soundness.} A scalar at the formula root reads $0=0$
(\cref{cert:def:reading}), so the initial context is a theorem and a scalar
goal is trivially derivable; no invariant on the roots of goals is needed.
The induction is that of \cref{cert:thm:base-sound}, carried out in $\Teq$.
The tree $M^{\circ}$ is an array in every case. Hence the test of step (3)
succeeds only for a nonempty array $m$ that is $[s,u]$ with $s$ true-valued,
or $[t,t,t]$ with $s$ false-valued; a scalar major never passes it. In the
first case the major reads $\sigma=\upsilon$ and the replacement is a
transport of equals, valid in $\Teq$. In the second the major reads
$\Q{y}B(y)$, the goal $B(\upsilon)$ is replaced by $B(0)$, and the reading
$B(0)\to\forall y\,B(y)$ of the closure justifies it. The openings are
evaluated at the caller's floor, which the major call does not change.

By the induction hypothesis the reading of $m$ is derivable. If $m=[B]$, the
exported hypothesis reads $\Q{y}B(b,y)$ with $b$ fresh. For a compound $B$
the auxiliary reading of $\D{x}{y}B$ is $\exists x$ of the reading of
$\Q{y}B(x,y)$, and existential elimination applies. For a literal equation
$B$ it is $\exists x\,\forall y\,(B(x,y)\to B(x,\Suc y))$, from which
$B(b,0)\to\forall y\,B(b,y)$ follows by induction for the equation $B(b,y)$.
If $m$ is an array of arity at least $2$, or a true-valued numeric scalar, the
exported hypothesis reads $0=0$. If the threefold repetition of $m$ is
false-valued, the packet is a computation, treated by
\cref{cert:lem:zeroinstance,cert:lem:supply} in $\Teq$, or an introduction.
In an introduction the hypothesis is $B(\tau,z)$ exactly when the opened
matrix has arity $2$. For a literal equation the premise gives
$\forall y\,(B(\tau,y)\to B(\tau,\Suc y))$, the auxiliary reading with witness
$\tau$. For a matrix of arity $1$ or $3$ the premise gives $B(\tau,\Suc z)$
from $B(\tau,0)$ for fresh $z$, hence $B(\tau,0)\to\forall y\,B(\tau,y)$ by
coverage. For other arities the matrix reads $0=0$ and the conclusion is
valid outright. In the native protocol the
ordinary reading is used throughout and the case analysis is that of
\cref{cert:thm:base-sound}.

\emph{Completeness.} The induction of \cref{cert:thm:base-complete} is
repeated with local floors (\cref{cert:lem:local}) and the packets
\[
[[s,u],s,u,F],\qquad [[B,B,B],0,u,B],\qquad [[B],0,0,0],\qquad
[0,s,u,t],\qquad [0,0,0,w]
\]
for \rulename{SE} at a source other than the literal $0$, \rulename{SE} at the
literal $0$, \rulename{DE}, a computation and the introduction; in the native
protocol the introduction by cases has $u=[\,]$. The canonical encoding of a
source other than the literal $0$ is true-valued, so $M^{\circ}$ is the
intended major in both cases of \rulename{SE}, and for \rulename{DE} the unary
major differs from $M^{\circ}=[0,0,0]$. A neutral major $0$ is closed by the
initial member of the context without a packet. Computations with a literal
zero initializer or predecessor are first replaced as in
\cref{cert:thm:certzero}; the transport used there has the source $C$, which
is not the literal $0$, so the replacement is available in $\mathcal U$ and
$\mathcal A^{-}$. A canonical goal is a nonempty array and is not closed by
the scalar member of the context.
\end{proof}

By \cref{cert:rem:imported}, for a canonical public formula $A$ the checker of
the guarded-source protocol accepts some stack with goal $A$ if and only if
$A$ is a theorem of the public theory. The checker also accepts goals that
are not canonical public formulas, for example scalars and auxiliary formulas;
the compact programs assume that the goal offered to them is canonical, and
this assumption is their input contract (\cref{cert:sec:validated}).

\begin{remark}[Comparison with the empty-array premise]\label{cert:rem:scalar-vs-empty}
The predecessor programs have the initial context $[[\,]]$ and the neutral
major $[\,]$. The scalar spelling is shorter by one byte and one syntax-tree
node. It does not need the invariant of \cref{cert:lem:nonumeric}: in the
test of step (3) the candidate $M^{\circ}$ is always an array, whereas the
test of \cref{cert:def:neutral} has a Boolean candidate that a numeric zero
matches. The two spellings do not accept the same raw stacks, since a stack
with the old neutral major can be rejected. The translation of certificates
replaces the major field of a packet when that field is exactly the empty
array and changes no other occurrence of an empty array; in particular the
mode field of an introduction in the native protocol is kept.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Substitution in the restricted calculus]
In the soundness proof the fresh names of a generated computation equation
are specialized to zero by \cref{cert:lem:supply} applied in $\Teq$, after
the continuation has been interpreted there. Applying a substitution theorem
to derivations of $\mathcal U$ would be incorrect, as noted in
\cref{cert:rem:imported}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Loops over immutable contexts}

\begin{theorem}[Tail call and loop]\label{cert:thm:loop}
In any of the protocols of this chapter, replace the final call of step (6) by
a return to step (1) with the new goal and the new context, and represent
contexts by immutable tuples that are extended by forming a new tuple. The
