checking function has no default value, and the initial call
$c([0],q())$ is replaced by $c([0],{*}q())$, so that the last element of the
input is a pair $[A,k_0]$ of the goal and an initial floor.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item For every stack $\sigma$ with last element $A$, let $\sigma'$ be $\sigma$
with its last element replaced by $[A,0]$. Then $H$ accepts $\sigma'$ exactly
when the program of $479$ bytes accepts $\sigma$, with the same run.
\item Let the input consist of integers and arrays and let $A$ be the canonical
encoding of a closed public formula of $\mathcal U$. If $H$ accepts an input
with last element $[A,k_0]$, then $k_0$ is an integer, $k_0\le 0$, and $A$ is a
theorem of $\mathcal U$.
\end{enumerate}
Hence $H$ and the program of $479$ bytes accept the same canonical goals.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) The program of $479$ bytes enters $c([0],A,0)$ with its default floor, and
$H$ enters the same call with the same remaining stack. The bodies are
identical.

(b) The checking function has exactly three positional parameters, so the
last element must be an array with exactly two elements; otherwise the call
fails. In particular the header cannot add a member to the initial context
$[0]$. The first operation is the opening of $A$ at the floor $k_0$; unless it
has failed earlier, it reaches the scalar $0$ of \cref{cert:lem:guarded-zero}
at an odd depth $d\ge 3$ and compares it with $k_0$. If $k_0$ is an array, the
comparison fails. If $k_0>0$, then $0\notin[k_0,d)$, the depth is odd, the
missing image, the checking function, is consulted, and the array test fails
on it. In both cases the run rejects before a packet is read. So $k_0$ is an
integer and $k_0\le 0$, a floor in the sense of \cref{cert:sec:codec}. The
program of $479$ bytes has, call by call, the runs of the program (iv) of
\cref{cert:prop:compositions} (\cref{cert:prop:immediate}(a) and
\cref{cert:prop:truthy-first}), and the soundness statement of
\cref{cert:prop:scalar} for that program is proved for an arbitrary floor and
an arbitrary context whose members are closed at that floor; the scalar $0$
is closed at every floor. So the auxiliary
reading of $A$ at the floor $k_0$ is derivable in $\Teq$ from $0=0$. The
canonical encoding $A$ contains no negative scalar, so it is closed at the
floor $0$, and by \cref{cert:lem:fresh} its reading at the floor $k_0$ is the
closed formula that it encodes; the parameters with names in $[k_0,0)$ carry no
assumption and do not occur in it. By \cref{cert:rem:imported} the formula is
a theorem of $\mathcal U$. The converse is (a) with the completeness of
\cref{cert:prop:compositions}(iv).
\end{proof}

The program, of \pkg{zero\_test\_split}, has $271/305/478$ against
$272/304/479$: deleting the default saves two bytes, the unpacking costs one
byte and one node. Its input format differs from that of its control in the
last element, and an untrusted converter wraps an old goal as $[A,0]$; with a
nonpositive offset $-r$ it also shifts every negative name of a canonical
certificate by $-r$, and the package proves for canonical certificates that
openings and allocation commute with this shift. The argument of (b) uses the
scalar $0$ of a canonical goal, and the program does not validate its goal; it
is a program of the raw contract with a canonical goal assumed.

\begin{remark}[The header in the native programs]\label{cert:rem:header-native}
The same change applied to the programs of $607$ and $637$ bytes (giving $606$
and $636$ bytes) is unsound, and neither program is delivered. The native
language has closed formulas without the scalar $0$, such as
$\D{x}{y}E(y)$ with
\[
E(y)\colon\quad \It{y}{y}{u}{\Suc u}=\It{y}{y}{u}{\It{y}{u}{v}{\Suc v}},
\]
which says $2y=y+y^{2}$; it holds at $0$ and $1$ and fails at $2$, so its
ordinary reading $E(0)\to\forall y\,E(y)$ is false. At the initial floor $1$
the first name allocated is the scalar $0$, which reads as the constant $0$ and
not as a fresh parameter, so the premise of an introduction by progress at
this name becomes $E(0)\proves E(\Suc 0)$, whose conclusion is a true closed
equation. \pkg{zero\_test\_split} reports a certificate
of $30$ packets that both modified programs accept and that the unmodified
native program and the program of $478$ bytes at the floor $0$ reject; the
certificate is not displayed in its report (\cref{cert:sec:questions}).
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Compositions of the preceding rewritings}

\pkg{checked\_reductions} and the second delivery of \pkg{refinement} combine
the rewritings of \cref{cert:prop:parity,cert:prop:bit-products,cert:prop:gate-or,cert:prop:eager-selector,cert:prop:header,cert:prop:inverted};
their arithmetic parts belong to another chapter. The proofs of these
propositions use, for the parity comparison and the product of bits, only that
the leaf condition is evaluated in the first opening at the floor $0$ on exact
integers and afterwards either not at all or inside a guard that holds
whatever its value; for the disjunctive gate only the line that binds the
removal function; for the eager selector only the definedness of $e$ on
canonical certificates; and for the header only the first opening of a
canonical guarded goal.

\begin{proposition}[Compositions]\label{cert:prop:late-compositions}
Each of the following programs has exactly the runs of its control, except
(i), whose relation to its control is that of \cref{cert:prop:eager-selector}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The program of $628$ bytes (\cref{cert:prop:parity}) with the gate of
\cref{cert:prop:gate-or} (\pkg{checked\_reductions}, $340/385/623$).
\item The phase by an empty context of \cref{cert:prop:empty-phase}
($334/387/654$) with the parity comparison, the gate of
\cref{cert:prop:gate-or} and the inverted entry test of
\cref{cert:prop:inverted} (\pkg{checked\_reductions}, $330/379/641$; without the
inverted entry test $330/380/640$, an intermediate program of the package).
\item The program of $607$ bytes (\cref{cert:prop:native-int}) with the parity
comparison (\pkg{checked\_reductions}, $336/378/600$).
\item The program of $629$ bytes with the parity comparison
(\pkg{checked\_reductions} and \pkg{refinement}, with identical text,
$323/369/622$).
\item The program of $629$ bytes with the parity comparison and the product of
bits $e\cdot(\text{exact-integer test}\land P)$ (\pkg{refinement},
$321/371/618$).
\item The program of $607$ bytes with the same product (\pkg{refinement},
$334/380/596$).
\item The phase by an empty context with the parity comparison, the product of
bits and the gate of \cref{cert:prop:gate-or} (\pkg{refinement},
$330/380/638$).
\item The program of $628$ bytes, that is, the program of $637$ bytes with the
parity comparison, with the product of bits and the gate of
\cref{cert:prop:gate-or} (\pkg{refinement}, $340/385/621$).
\item The program of $478$ bytes of \pkg{zero\_test\_split}
(\cref{cert:prop:header}) with the eager selector of
\cref{cert:prop:eager-selector} (\pkg{checked\_reductions}, $273/305/477$).
\end{enumerate}
The programs (a), (b), (g) and (h) satisfy the typed contract for the language
with equality and $D$, the programs (c)--(f) the goal contract, and (i) the
raw contract with a canonical guarded goal assumed and the header of
\cref{cert:prop:header}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The rewritings act on different expressions, so it suffices to check that each
hypothesis used in its proof holds in the composed program.

\emph{Parity and products.} In the programs with the phase by context ((b),
(d), (e), (g)) the leaf condition stands inside ``$g$ or \dots'' and is
evaluated only while the context is empty, in the first opening, at the floor
$0$; the range test gives $a\ge 0$, and $a$ is an exact integer by the test of
the printed form ((b), (g)) or by the exact-integer conjunct that precedes the
parity comparison ((d), (e)). In the programs with the flag ((a), (c), (f),
(h)) the condition is the guarded condition of $v\le p$, which holds whatever
$p$ is when $v=0$; when $v=1$ the floor is $0$ and the leaf is an exact
integer, by the printed form ((a), (h)) or by the exact-integer conjunct ((c),
(f)). In the products of (e) and (f) the factor that is now evaluated also when
$e=0$ is the conjunction of the exact-integer test with the parity comparison;
the exact-integer test was already evaluated first in the controls, it is
defined on every number that passes the range test, and the parity comparison
is evaluated only after it has succeeded, on an exact integer. In (g) and (h)
the factor is the parity comparison on an exact integer. So the identity of
\cref{cert:prop:parity} and the argument of \cref{cert:prop:bit-products}
apply, the values of the guards agree, and no new operation fails.

\emph{Gate and entry test.} \Cref{cert:prop:gate-or} concerns only the binding
of the removal function before the first call, and \cref{cert:prop:inverted}
only the form of the entry test in a procedure whose body ends with the final
call, which is the form of the program of $654$ bytes.

\emph{Header and eager selector.} With the header $[A,0]$ the program of $478$
bytes of \pkg{zero\_test\_split} has the runs of the program of $479$ bytes, and
\cref{cert:prop:header}(b) shows that an accepted header has a floor
$k_0\le 0$. The proof of \cref{cert:prop:eager-selector}(a) is a replacement of
selectors in a returning run and does not depend on the initial floor; so every
input accepted by (i) is accepted by its control with the same run, and the
soundness of \cref{cert:prop:header} applies. The certificates of
\cref{cert:prop:scalar}, with the last element $[A,0]$, are accepted by (i) by
\cref{cert:prop:eager-selector}(b). The stack of
\cref{cert:prop:eager-selector}(c) with its last element written $[E,0]$ is
accepted by the control and rejected by (i).
\end{proof}

\subsubsection*{Zero as a defined alias in the public goal}

\pkg{zero\_alias} changes the encoding of public goals. By
\cref{cert:prop:reserved} the scalar $1$ at a term position behaves in the raw
protocol as a constant $c$ about which nothing is known, and
\cref{cert:cor:reserved} shows that a validator that merely admits it as a
spelling of zero loses completeness. The remark after that corollary names an
identification of spellings as a possible repair whose cost must be counted;
\pkg{zero\_alias} implements it by an assumption in the initial context.

\begin{definition}[Zero-alias encoding and checker]\label{cert:def:zero-alias}
For a formula $B$ of the language with equality and $D$ let $B^{\sharp}$ be its
canonical encoding with every scalar $0$ at a term position replaced by the
scalar $1$; let $P^{\mathrm{ed}}_{1}$ be the predicate $P^{\mathrm{ed}}$ of
\cref{cert:def:public} with the leaf alternative ``$a=0$, or $a$ odd with
$3\le a<e$'' replaced by ``$a$ odd with $1\le a<e$''. The \emph{zero-alias
checker} is the program (v) of \cref{cert:prop:compositions} with the guards
of \cref{cert:prop:order}, the reset of the flag in the major call of
\cref{cert:prop:native-first}, the inverted entry test of
\cref{cert:prop:inverted} and the gate of \cref{cert:prop:gate-or}, with the
leaf condition of the flagged opener replaced by $e\mathbin{\&}a$ (the depth
is odd and $a$ is odd), and with the initial context $[0,[1,0]]$ instead of
$[0]$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Zero as a defined alias]\label{cert:prop:zero-alias}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The zero-alias checker accepts only inputs that satisfy $J$ and whose goal
$a$ satisfies $P^{\mathrm{ed}}_{1}(a,0)$; in particular it accepts no goal
that contains the scalar $0$ at a term position.
\item If it accepts an input with goal $a$, then the sorted reading of $a$, in
which the scalar $1$ reads as $0$, is a closed theorem of $\mathcal A^{-}$, and
the parametric reading of \cref{cert:prop:reserved}, in which $1$ reads as
$c_1$, is derivable in $\mathcal A^{-}$ over the language with $c_1$ from
$c_1=0$.
\item For every closed theorem $B$ of $\mathcal A^{-}$ in the language with
equality and $D$, the checker accepts some certificate with goal $B^{\sharp}$.
\end{enumerate}
Hence the accepted canonical goals are exactly the encodings $B^{\sharp}$ of the
closed theorems $B$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) The gate gives $J$ (\cref{cert:prop:gate-or,cert:thm:repr}). In the first
opening, with the flag $1$ and the floor $0$, a leaf at an odd depth $d$ is
retained exactly when $0\le a<d$ and $a$ is odd, that is, $a$ odd with
$1\le a<d$; at an even depth the guarded condition is $0$, and the fallback
consults the missing image and rejects. The arity conditions are those of the
control. The argument of \cref{cert:lem:scope} gives $P^{\mathrm{ed}}_{1}$, and
the opening returns the goal unchanged.

(b) After the first opening the program is the kernel of the program (v), whose
runs are not changed by the devices listed, and its soundness statement
(\cref{cert:prop:context-first}(a) in its native form) holds for an arbitrary
initial context of trees closed at the floor $0$. The member $[1,0]$ is closed
at every floor, since $1$ lies in the range of every term position, and its
sorted reading is $0=0$; so the sorted reading of the goal is derivable from
$0=0$, hence outright. For the parametric reading, \cref{cert:prop:reserved}
gives derivability from the parametric readings of the context, which are
$0=0$ and $c_1=0$.

(c) Let $z$ be a parameter and $F(v)$ the template obtained from $B$ by writing
$v$ for each occurrence of the constant $0$ at a term position, so that
$B=F(0)$. In $\mathcal A$ (\cref{cert:def:A}), \rulename{Tr} accepts every source,
including $0$: from $z=0$ it derives $0=z$ (transport with the template
$v=z$ and the reflexive $z=z$), and then $F(z)$ from $F(0)$, which is derivable
since $B$ is a theorem. So $z=0\proves F(z)$ in $\mathcal A$, hence in
$\mathcal A^{-}$ (\cref{cert:rem:imported}). Its eigenparameters are fresh, so
they differ from $z$, and a source $z$ of \rulename{SE} is not the literal $0$.
Compile this derivation by the completeness proof of the program
(v) (\cref{cert:prop:compositions}, with \cref{cert:prop:context-first}(b)),
with $z$ encoded by the scalar $1$ in place of a negative name. The compilation
uses of a parameter only that it is retained by every opening, is never the hole
and never equals a fresh name; the scalar $1$ has these properties, since every
term position has depth at least $3$ and fresh names are negative
(\cref{cert:prop:reserved}). The source field $1$ is true-valued, as the
encoding of a parameter source is. The hypothesis $z=0$ is the member $[1,0]$
of the initial context. The resulting certificate proves the goal $F(1)$ in the
encoding, which is $B^{\sharp}$; it satisfies $P^{\mathrm{ed}}_{1}$, so the
first opening passes, and the run is that of the kernel.
\end{proof}
% ed.: the package states completeness in one sentence ("compile a reference
% proof, then use derived formula transport under c=0"); the derivation in A
% with the parameter z, the passage to A^- and the encoding of z by the scalar
% 1 are supplied here.

The program, of \pkg{zero\_alias} (\texttt{implementation/alias\_inverted.py}),
has $331/373/625$; its readable companion has $430/499/2322$. Its contract is
not that of T/ed: it accepts the encodings $B^{\sharp}$ and rejects every
canonical goal of T/ed that contains the scalar $0$, so it satisfies neither
the completeness of T/ed nor the converse. It is listed under its own code. The
package states that it is below the envelope $334/386/628$ of T/ed in all three
coordinates, which is true of the figures and is a comparison across contracts
related by the bijection $B\mapsto B^{\sharp}$ on goals; completeness is claimed
for canonical encodings only, as under T/ed. The package's negative control,
the same program with the initial context $[0]$, rejects a translated
certificate of a computation, as \cref{cert:cor:reserved} predicts.

\subsubsection*{Erasing the alphabet precondition}

\pkg{tolerant\_certificates} removes the last assumption on certificates from a
goal-validated program. The programs of the goal contract recognize arrays by
$a\cdot 0\neq 0$, which is true for strings as well; their soundness is stated
for consumed packets that are integers and arrays, and a nonempty string at an
active position makes an ideal execution traverse it without end, as noted in
\cref{cert:prop:native-int}(b). The package replaces the test by
$a\cdot 0=[\,]$, which on values of the JSON reader holds exactly for arrays,
is false for numbers and strings, and fails for \texttt{null} and objects.

\begin{definition}[Numeric respelling; tolerant contract]\label{cert:def:tolerant}
A \emph{numeric respelling} of a raw tree replaces leaves by values of the JSON
reader with the same numerical value: integral finite floats, and, for the
leaf $0$, also \texttt{False} or the negative floating zero. A checker
satisfies the \emph{tolerant goal contract} for a grammar if every input
accepted by the JSON reader leads to a terminating ideal run, every accepted
goal is a numeric respelling of a canonical closed formula of the grammar whose
reading is a theorem, and every numeric respelling of the canonical encoding of
a closed theorem is accepted with some certificate. No property of unused or
consumed certificate data is assumed.
\end{definition}

Let $\widetilde N$ be the program (e) of \cref{cert:prop:late-compositions}
($321/371/618$) with the exact-integer conjunct deleted from its leaf
condition and with the array test $a\cdot 0\neq 0$ replaced by $a\cdot 0=[\,]$.

\begin{lemma}[First opening of $\widetilde N$]\label{cert:lem:tolerant-goal}
The first opening of $\widetilde N$, with the empty context and the floor $0$,
is defined exactly when the goal is a numeric respelling of a tree $b$ with
$P^{\mathrm{ed}}(b,0)$, and it then returns the goal unchanged.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The array test separates arrays from all other values of the reader, and the
arity condition is that of the control. A string reaches the scalar branch and
fails the range test; \texttt{null} and objects fail the array test. A numeric
leaf passes exactly when $0\le a<d$, the depth $d$ is odd, and
$a\bmod 2=[a>1]$. For a finite nonnegative number the last condition holds
exactly at $0$ and at the odd integers of at least $3$: for $0<a<2$ the
remainder is $a$, which is neither $0$ nor $1$ when $a\neq 1$, while $[a>1]$ is
$0$ or $1$, and at $a=1$ the two sides are $1$ and $0$; for $a\ge 2$ the side
$[a>1]$ is $1$, and the floating remainder modulo $2$ is exact, so it is $1$
exactly at the odd integers; \texttt{True} has the value $1$ and fails,
\texttt{False} has the value $0$ and passes. Infinite values and NaN fail the range test. Every
failure consults the missing image, the checking function, and fails. The
opening retains every leaf it admits, so it returns the goal. Induction on the
goal gives the claim, as in \cref{cert:lem:scope}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Later openings of $\widetilde N$]\label{cert:lem:tolerant-open}
After the first opening, every opening of $\widetilde N$ terminates; it fails
when it reaches a string, \texttt{null} or an object; a nonfinite number behaves
as a scalar outside every range, that is, as the hole at a term position and as
$0=0$ at a formula position; and a returning opening has the properties of
\cref{cert:lem:opening,cert:lem:exposure,cert:lem:fresh} for the raw trees of
\cref{cert:def:raw}, whose numeric scalars include floats and Booleans.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
With a nonempty context the arity condition is inactive, and the array test
recognizes exactly arrays. A string reaches the range test and fails; it is no
longer traversed. \texttt{null} and objects fail the array test. A nonfinite
number fails the range test and is replaced by the image at an odd depth or by
the scalar $0$ at an even depth, which is the treatment of a hole. Traversal is
structural on a finite tree, and the image is opened once with the missing image,
after which a further hole fails; so every opening terminates. A returning
opening has met only arrays and finite or nonfinite numbers at the positions it
traversed, and the proofs of the three lemmas apply verbatim; for freshness,
finitely many names lie in $[k-1,k)$, the case treated after
\cref{cert:lem:fresh}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Erasure of the alphabet precondition]\label{cert:thm:tolerant}
$\widetilde N$ satisfies the tolerant goal contract for the language with
equality and $D$ and the calculus $\mathcal A^{-}$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item on every input accepted by the JSON reader its ideal run terminates;
\item if it accepts, the goal is a numeric respelling of a tree $b$ with
$P^{\mathrm{ed}}(b,0)$ whose reading is a closed theorem of $\mathcal A^{-}$;
\item for every closed theorem $B$ with canonical encoding $b$ and every numeric
respelling $b'$ of $b$, some certificate with goal $b'$ is accepted.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) The input must be an array, or the removal of the goal fails. Every call
that does not return at its membership test removes a packet from the finite
stack before any recursive call, and every opening terminates
(\cref{cert:lem:tolerant-goal,cert:lem:tolerant-open}). So the tree of calls is
finite.

(b) By \cref{cert:lem:tolerant-goal} the goal has the stated form. For the run
after the first opening, the soundness proof of the control (the program of
$629$ bytes, \cref{cert:prop:native-composition}, with
\cref{cert:prop:native-append,cert:prop:late-compositions}) is an induction on a
returning run over raw trees; it uses only that the operations it relies on are
defined, and by \cref{cert:lem:tolerant-open} an opening that reaches a value
other than an array or a number fails. The remaining places where such a value
could enter are these. A packet that is a string or an object of four entries
is destructured into strings, and its major, a string, fails at the opening that
begins the major call. The comparison of the major with $[s,u]$ or $[t,t,t]$ and
the membership test compare a fully opened tree, which contains only arrays and
numbers, with another tree; equal trees then contain only arrays and numbers,
and equal numbers have equal readings, so the identities used by the soundness
proof hold. A field that is never opened or compared has no role in the
induction. In an introduction the mode field $u$ is false-valued, and the test
$\lvert a\rvert\bmod 2=u$ selects progress only if $u$ is numerically $0$; no
false-valued value equals $1$, so a compound matrix of arity $1$ or $3$ never
receives progress. A false-valued source used as an image must open to the
reading $0$; numeric zeros and $[\,]$ do, and other false-valued values fail if
they are used. The extra copies of $0$ in the contexts of callers
(\cref{cert:prop:native-append}) read $0=0$. Hence the reading of the goal is
derivable.

(c) On inputs of integers and arrays the two array tests agree, and the deleted
conjunct is true on exact integers, so $\widetilde N$ has the runs of its
control; the canonical certificate of $B$ (\cref{cert:prop:native-composition})
is accepted with goal $b$. With goal $b'$ relate trees of equal shape whose
leaves are numerically equal. The range tests, the parity condition, the truth
values, the equalities and the membership tests of the program have the same
values on related trees, and openings map related trees to related trees; the
goal is not multiplied, decremented or tested for its type. So the run with
goal $b'$ is related to the run with goal $b$ step by step and accepts.
\end{proof}

The program, of \pkg{tolerant\_certificates} (\texttt{code/checker.py}), has
$315/361/603$ against $321/371/618$ for its control under the goal contract. It
satisfies neither the goal contract, since it accepts respelled goals, nor the
typed contract, since it does not test the alphabet; and the programs of those
contracts do not satisfy its contract: the programs with the array test
$a\cdot 0\neq 0$ do not terminate, in ideal execution, on some inputs with a
nonempty string at an active position (in an actual run they reject at the
recursion limit), and their soundness is not established for consumed data that
are not integers and arrays. It is listed under its own code. The package
states that it is below the envelope $330/380/621$ of the typed contract in all
three coordinates; this compares different contracts. Completeness is claimed,
as elsewhere, for canonical encodings and their respellings and not for every
spelling with references to dummy slots.

\subsubsection*{Two fields for the conditional-copy calculus}

\pkg{alias\_back} gives, besides a further delivery of the program of $479$
bytes (\cref{cert:rem:repeated-deliveries}), a certificate format for the
calculus $\mathsf K$ of conditional copy (\pkg{conditional\_copy}; the calculus,
its terms with the counter-aware recursor in full normal form, its clauses and
its rules \rulename{Use}, \rulename{Close} and \rulename{Export} are those of
the chapter on witnesses, where its tree checker is measured). The tree
checker reads a pair $[A,p]$ of a goal and a proof tree, infers the formula
proved by $p$ in the initial context $g_0$ of the reflexivity clause, and
compares it with $A$: a node $[j,t,q]$ uses the clause $j$ at the term $t$,
with $q$ a proof of its antecedent, ignored when the antecedent is empty; a
node $[B,r]$ with $B$ nonempty is \rulename{Close} for the goal $[B]$; a node
$[[\,],q,r]$ is \rulename{Export}, with $q$ a proof of a formula $[D]$ and $r$ a
proof in the context extended by the opened clause. Every active term and
formula is validated for exact integers and arrays, sort, scope and normal
form.

\begin{definition}[Two-field stream for $\mathsf K$]\label{cert:def:k-stream}
A certificate is an array whose last element is the goal and whose earlier
elements are pairs, consumed from the end; the whole array must be consumed.
The goal-directed procedure $\mathrm{Prove}(A,g)$ validates $A$ as a normal
closed formula in the scope of $g$, removes a pair and
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item for $[j,t]$ with $j$ an integer, $0\le j<\lvert g\rvert$: validates $t$,
runs $\mathrm{Prove}$ on the antecedent of the clause $j$ if it is nonempty,
and requires the normalized instance of the clause at $t$ to be $A$;
\item for $[[\,],[\,]]$: requires $A=[B]$ and computes the matrix, the new
hypothesis and the successor instance from $B$ and $\lvert g\rvert$ exactly as
the tree checker does for $[B,r]$, and runs $\mathrm{Prove}$ on the successor
instance in the extended context;
\item for $[B,F]$ with $B$ nonempty: runs $\mathrm{Prove}([B],g)$, opens $B$ at
the new parameter as the tree checker does for \rulename{Export}, runs
$\mathrm{Prove}(F,g')$ in the extended context $g'$, and requires the
replacement of the tree checker, applied once to $F$, to give $A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Two fields for $\mathsf K$]\label{cert:prop:k-stream}
For a context $g$ reachable from $g_0$ and a formula $A$, a call
$\mathrm{Prove}(A,g)$ returns after consuming a finite segment of the stack
exactly when there is a proof tree $p$ on which the tree checker, in the
context $g$, infers $A$. Hence the two checkers accept the same goals.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
From a returning stream call to a tree, by induction on the call, which is
finite since every call removes a pair. For $[j,t]$ the tree is $[j,t,q]$ with
$q$ the tree given by the induction hypothesis for the antecedent, or any value
if the antecedent is empty; the tree checker computes the instance at $t$,
which the stream checker has compared with $A$, and it validates that instance,
which the stream checker has validated as $A$. For $[[\,],[\,]]$ the goal is
$[B]$ and the tree is $[B,r]$ with $r$ the tree for the successor instance;
both checkers perform the same substitutions and the same test of the zero
instance, which decides between the hypothesis $B(b)$ and the zero instance,
and extend the context by the same clause. For $[B,F]$ the tree is
$[[\,],q,r]$ with $q$ the tree for $[B]$ and $r$ the tree for $F$; the tree
checker infers $[B]$ from $q$, opens $B$ at the same parameter, retains the
same clause, infers $F$ from $r$ and applies the same replacement, whose result
is $A$; its final validation of $A$ is the validation that the stream checker
performs at entry.

From a tree to a stream, by induction on the tree: emit $[j,t]$ followed by
the stream of the antecedent, $[[\,],[\,]]$ followed by the stream of the
successor instance, and $[B,F]$, with $[B]$ and $F$ the formulas inferred for
$q$ and $r$, followed by the stream of $q$ and then that of $r$, in the order in
which the stream checker consumes them. The same computations succeed. At the
root, reverse the emitted pairs and append $A$; the stream checker consumes
the whole array.
\end{proof}

The tree format admits an ignored field and the stream format does not, and an
\rulename{Export} pair carries the two formulas that the tree checker infers,
so a stream can be longer than the tree; no statement about the size of
certificates is made. The stream checker has $609/699/1644$
(\texttt{supplement/two\_field/code/two\_fields\_compact.py}). Its control
($629/732/1715$) is the tree checker of \pkg{conditional\_copy} with the same
stylistic simplifications, constructed by the package; the delivered tree
checker has $646/748/1844$. Untrusted converters translate in both directions,
and each translated proof is replayed by the other checker.

\subsection{Two fields, and computation equations recognized from the goal}\label{cert:sec:two-field}

This subsection follows the two-field protocol of \pkg{refinement} and its
independent review, which the package includes. It changes the format of
certificates and does not improve a size: computation equations consume no
packet, and every other packet has two fields.

\begin{definition}[The calculus $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$]\label{cert:def:Utr}
$\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ is $\mathcal U$ (\cref{cert:def:U}) with
\rulename{SE} replaced by \rulename{Tr} and \rulename{QE}. Equivalently, it is
$\mathcal A$ (\cref{cert:def:A}) without the instances of \rulename{DI}-case
whose matrix is a literal equation.
\end{definition}

The two descriptions agree: both calculi have \rulename{Hyp}, \rulename{I0},
\rulename{IS}, \rulename{Tr}, \rulename{QE} and \rulename{DE}, and the
introduction of $\mathcal U$ is \rulename{DI}-progress at a literal equation
and \rulename{DI}-case at a compound matrix. Every rule of
$\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ is a rule of $\mathcal A$, and \rulename{SE} is an
instance of \rulename{QE} when its source is the literal $0$ and of
\rulename{Tr} otherwise. Hence every public sequent derivable in $\mathcal U$
is derivable in $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$, and every sequent derivable in
$\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ is derivable in $\mathcal A$. By
\cref{cert:rem:imported} and the equivalence of $\mathcal A$ with $\Nat$, a
closed public formula is a theorem of $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ exactly if
it is a theorem of $\mathcal U$, exactly if its ordinary reading is derivable
in $\Teq$.

\begin{definition}[Two-field protocol]\label{cert:def:two-field}
A packet is a pair $[m,t]$. A call with goal $a$, context $g$ and floor $k$,
initially $[0]$ and $0$, performs the following steps.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(T\arabic*)]
\item If $O_k(a;\bot,0)$ is a member of $g$, return.
\item If $a=[[n,b,h],r]$ with $n$ false-valued and $b=r$, return. If
$a=[[n,b,h],r]$ with $n=[n']$ and $O_k(h;[n',b,h],3)=r$, return. A failed
operation in this step does not reject; it passes to step (T3).
\item Remove the last packet $[m,t]$ and prove $m$ by a call with context $g$
and floor $k$.
\item If $m=[s,u]$ is binary, require $O_k(t;u,0)=a$ and replace $a$ by
$O_k(t;s,0)$. If $m$ is ternary with last entry $B$, require $O_k(B;t,0)=a$
and replace $a$ by $O_k(B;0,0)$. If $m$ is a true-valued scalar, reject.
\item If $m$ is true-valued, let $h$ be the threefold repetition of $m$.
Otherwise replace $a$ by the opening $O_k(a_0;t,2)$ of its single child $a_0$
at the witness $t$, and let $h\defeq a$.
\item Decrease the floor to $k-1$ and call recursively with the goal
$O_{k-1}(a;[k-1],0)$ and the context $g+[O_{k-1}(h;\iota,0)]$, where
$\iota=k-1$ if $m$ is true-valued or $a$ is binary, and $\iota=0$ otherwise.
\end{enumerate}
The checker accepts if the initial call on the goal returns. Its contract is
the raw contract with a canonical guarded goal assumed: finite trees of
integers and arrays, and claims about canonical closed guarded goals.
\end{definition}

Step (T2) takes the place of the computation branch of the four-field
protocols. It introduces no parameter, so the zero-instance supply of
\cref{cert:lem:supply,cert:lem:zeroinstance} is not used, and no false-valued
alias of zero is needed to select a mode.

\begin{lemma}[Erasure of dummy slots]\label{cert:lem:dummy-erasure}
Let a raw tree $a$ be validated at the term depth $3+2p$ and floor $k$. Then
at every term depth $3+2(p+q)$ at which the opening of $a$ is defined, the
reading of $a$, with the $q$ additional outer slots read as $0$, is its
reading at depth $3+2p$ with the $p$ slots read as $0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By induction on $a$. Below $r$ binders of $a$ itself, a scalar with a
reading lies in $[k,3+2(p+r))$ at the shallower depth, because $a$ is
validated there; at the deeper depth it lies in the same interval and refers
to the same object: $0$, a parameter, one of the $r$ inner slots, which are
innermost in both readings, or one of the $p$ outer slots, read as $0$; it
never refers to one of the $q$ added slots. Annotations have no reading. At a
unary or ternary term array the induction hypothesis applies to the children
below their prescribed slots.
\end{proof}

The lemma makes no claim when an outer slot that is present in both readings
carries a value other than $0$. Below, the zero computation uses it with
$p=0$, and the successor computation with $p=1$.

\begin{lemma}[Computation leaves]\label{cert:lem:leaves}
Let $a$ be a goal validated at floor $k$ for which step (T2) returns. Then
the reading of $a$ is an instance of \rulename{I0} or of \rulename{IS}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The goal is an equation $[[n,b,h],r]$ whose left side is an iterator; its
children $n,b$ are read at depth $5$ below one dummy slot, $h$ at depth $5$
below the state slot, and $r$ at depth $3$. All of them are validated, since
$a$ is.

If $n$ is false-valued, then on trees of integers and arrays it is $0$ or the
empty array, which read as $0$. The tree $b$ equals $r$, which is validated
at depth $3$; by \cref{cert:lem:dummy-erasure} with $p=0$ and $q=1$, the
reading of $b$ at depth $5$ is the reading of $r$. So $a$ reads as
$\It{0}{\beta}{u}{\eta}=\beta$.

If $n=[n']$, the left side reads as $\It{\Suc\nu'}{\beta}{u}{\eta}$, where
$\nu'$ is the reading of $n'$ at depth $9$, below the dummy slot of the
counter and the two dummy slots of the successor. Suppose first that the
state occurs in the reading $\eta$ of $h$. Then the opening
$O_k(h;[n',b,h],3)$ validates its image at the root term depth $3$, so $n'$,
$b$ and $h$ are validated at depth $5$ as children of the image. By
\cref{cert:lem:dummy-erasure} with $p=1$ and $q=2$ the reading of $n'$ at
depth $9$ is its reading $\nu'$ at depth $5$, so the image reads as
$\It{\nu'}{\beta}{u}{\eta}$, and by \cref{cert:lem:exposure} the opening reads
as $\eta[\It{\nu'}{\beta}{u}{\eta}/u]$. This second validation is necessary:
a scalar of $n'$ that is admitted only because of the two dummy slots of the
successor would otherwise escape into the predecessor term. If the state does
not occur in $\eta$, the opening reads as $\eta$, which does not depend on the
state, and the equation
$\It{\Suc\nu'}{\beta}{u}{\eta}=\eta$ is the instance of \rulename{IS} for
every $\nu'$. In both cases the opening equals $r$ as a tree, and equal
trees at the same depth have the same reading; so $a$ is an instance of
\rulename{IS}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness of two fields]\label{cert:thm:two-field-sound}
If a call of the two-field protocol with goal $a$, context $g$ and floor $k$
returns, where $a$ and the members of $g$ are validated at floor $k$, then
the reading of $a$ is derivable in $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ from the
readings of the members of $g$. In particular a canonical closed public goal
that the checker accepts is a theorem of $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$, and its
ordinary reading is derivable in $\Teq$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By induction on the accepting call. In step (T1) the reading of $a$ is that of
a member of $g$ (\cref{cert:lem:opening}(b)), an instance of \rulename{Hyp}.
In step (T2) it is an axiom, by \cref{cert:lem:leaves}. Otherwise the major
call returns; its entry scan validates $m$ at floor $k$, and by the induction
hypothesis the reading $M$ of $m$ is derivable.

If $m=[s,u]$, then $M$ is $\sigma=\upsilon$; by \cref{cert:lem:opening}(c)
the goal $a=O_k(t;u,0)$ reads as $F(\upsilon)$ and the new goal $O_k(t;s,0)$
as $F(\sigma)$, for the reading $F$ of the template, and \rulename{Tr} gives
$F(\upsilon)$ from the major and $F(\sigma)$. If $m$ is ternary, $M$ is
$\Q{y}B'(y)$ for the reading $B'$ of its last child $B$, whose first two
entries are annotations and need not be copies of $B$; by
\cref{cert:lem:exposure} the goal reads as $B'(\tau)$ and the new goal as
$B'(0)$, and \rulename{QE} applies. In both cases the threefold repetition of
$m$ has arity $6$ or $9$ and reads $0=0$, a neutral resource
(\cref{cert:lem:neutral-resource}); the new goal is validated at floor $k$ by
\cref{cert:lem:opening}(a), so by \cref{cert:lem:fresh} its opening at floor
$k-1$ has the same reading. If $m$ is unary, $m=[B]$ reads as $\D{x}{y}B'$,
its repetition is the closure $[B,B,B]$, and its opening at the image $k-1$
and floor $k-1$ reads as $\Q{y}B'(b,y)$ with $b$ the parameter $k-1$
(\cref{cert:lem:exposure}); the goal is unchanged. By \cref{cert:lem:fresh}
the name $b$ does not occur in the readings of $g$, $a$ and $m$, all
validated at floor $k$, and \rulename{DE} applies. A major of arity at least
$4$ reads $0=0$ and leaves the goal unchanged, with a neutral resource.

If $m$ is false-valued, it reads $0=0$. A goal of arity other than $1$ that
reaches step (T5) either rejects at the unpacking of its children or reads as
$0=0$, which is derivable without assumptions. For $a=[a_0]$, reading as
$\D{x}{y}B$, the opening $O_k(a_0;t,2)$ reads as $B(\tau,y)$ with $y$ the
hole, where the witness $\tau$ is validated at floor $k$
(\cref{cert:lem:exposure}). The new goal reads as $B(\tau,\Suc z)$ with $z$
the parameter $k-1$, and the added hypothesis as $B(\tau,z)$ if the matrix is
an equation and as $B(\tau,0)$ otherwise. The name $z$ is fresh for $g$, for
$\tau$ and for $\D{x}{y}B$, which are validated at floor $k$. So the
introduction of $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$ applies; a matrix of arity $0$ or
at least $4$, or a scalar, reads $0=0$, and \rulename{DI}-case applies to it.
In every case the members of the extended context and the new goal are
validated at floor $k-1$, and the induction hypothesis applies to the final
call. The last statement follows from the equivalences after
\cref{cert:def:Utr}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Completeness with two fields]\label{cert:thm:two-field-complete}
For every canonical closed public formula that is a theorem of $\mathcal U$
there is a stack of two-field packets that the protocol accepts with that
goal.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cert:def:Utr} the formula has a derivation in $\mathcal U^{\mathrm{Tr}}$.
Compile it along the deterministic schedule of the checker, writing every
expression in its canonical encoding (\cref{cert:def:enc}) and naming the
eigenparameter of the inference at a call with floor $k$ by $k-1$. At a call
whose goal, after the entry scan, is a member of the context, or is
recognized by step (T2), emit no packet and discard the subderivation; the
names of later eigenparameters then follow the pruned tree of calls and not
the original derivation, so no packet is left in the stack to misalign a
later major or continuation. Otherwise emit the packet of the last inference
and compile the derivation of its major and then that of its continuation:
\rulename{Tr} with major $s=u$ and template $F$ gives $[[s,u],F]$, the
designated variable of $F$ encoded as the outermost slot of a formula read
below one slot; \rulename{QE} with major $\Q{y}B$ at the target $\tau$ gives
$[[B,B,B],\tau]$; \rulename{DE} with major $\D{x}{y}B$ gives $[[B],0]$; the
introduction at the witness $w$ gives $[0,w]$, whose major $0$ is proved by
membership in the initial context. The stack is the reverse of the emitted
sequence. A computation equation is written in the form that step (T2)
recognizes: for \rulename{I0} the seed is copied unchanged to the right side,
since the encoding of a term without free slots does not depend on the number
of slots above it, and for \rulename{IS} the counter is the successor array
of the predecessor and the right side is the opening of the update at the
recursive image, which is canonical by \cref{cert:lem:canonical}; a literal
zero predecessor is recognized directly. Since all parameters are named in
$[k,0)$ at a call with floor $k$, every opening of the run returns the
canonical encodings of \cref{cert:lem:canonical}, every test of step (T4)
succeeds, and the run follows the compiled derivation.
\end{proof}

Step (T2) need not recognize every raw spelling of a computation equation;
completeness needs only the canonical spellings. The entry scan of step (T1)
stays necessary: auxiliary certificates are checked by the program itself.
The certificates are not interchangeable with four-field certificates, and
the protocol has no control; its accepted canonical goals are those of every
checker of $\mathcal U$ in this chapter. The reference program of
\pkg{refinement} has $295/352/742$, above every minimum of
\cref{cert:sec:envelope}: the packets carry less data, but the checker has to
reconstruct the role of the major, and the package states that this does not
improve the size. The package supports the proof by finite evidence: of
$20{,}000$ generated candidate leaves, $4{,}065$ pass the entry scan and step
(T2), and all of them read as true equations under a sorted numerical
evaluator with one fixed assignment of the parameters; of $8{,}008$ further
candidates aimed at dummy slots and nested states, $3{,}608$ are accepted and
true and $4{,}400$ are rejected. The program runs $26$ of the $898$
executions of the package's suite.

\subsection{Computation leaves, uniform terminals and their compositions}\label{cert:sec:leaves}

This subsection draws on \pkg{computation\_leaves}, \pkg{shared\_leaf},
\pkg{uniform\_terminals}, \pkg{smaller\_presentations},
\pkg{vacuous\_specialization}, \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement},
\pkg{ast269\_successor\_rigidity}, \pkg{selective\_normalization},
\pkg{export\_specialization}, \pkg{476\_byte\_presentation},
\pkg{abstraction\_and\_refinements}, \pkg{checker\_refinements},
\pkg{orbit\_equality}, \pkg{fixed\_points\_and\_checker}, \pkg{offset472},
\pkg{scope\_refinement}, \pkg{validated\_leaves}, \pkg{ast261},
\pkg{checked\_presentations}, \pkg{root\_conversion\_raw},
\pkg{468\_shared\_leaf}, \pkg{shifted\_shared\_leaf}, \pkg{direct\_packets},
\pkg{procedure\_presentation} and \pkg{scalar\_constancy}, in their checker
parts. No rule is
changed: every program checks $\mathcal U$ under the raw contract with a
canonical guarded goal assumed, or $\mathcal A^{-}$ (equivalently
$\mathcal A$) under a goal or typed contract. The step (T2) of
\cref{cert:def:two-field}, which recognizes a computation equation as the goal
itself, is transferred to the three- and four-field formats.

\subsubsection*{The eager opener and the program of $477$ bytes of \pkg{head\_conversion}}

Several programs below open with the \emph{eager} opener $E_k$, which differs
from \cref{cert:def:opener} in its fallback: an out-of-range scalar at depth
$d$ is replaced by $(d\bmod 2)\cdot E_k(t;\bot,3)$, the product of the parity
with the opened image. At an odd depth this is the opened image; at an even
depth it is the scalar $0$ or the empty array, according as the opened image
is a scalar or an array; and the missing image is a function object, on which
the array test $a\cdot 0\neq 0$ fails.

\begin{lemma}[Eager opener]\label{cert:lem:eager}
If $E_k(a;t,d)$ is defined, then $O_k(a;t,d)$ is defined and the two trees
have the same reading at every position with a reading; if $t=\bot$, then
$E_k(a;\bot,d)=a$. Hence \cref{cert:lem:opening,cert:lem:exposure,cert:lem:fresh}
and \cref{cert:lem:leaves} hold with $E_k$ in place of $O_k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By induction on $a$. The two openers traverse arrays at the same depths. A
scalar in range is retained by both. For an out-of-range scalar $E_k$
evaluates $E_k(t;\bot,3)$, which fails for $t=\bot$; so with the missing
image every scalar is in range and $E_k(a;\bot,d)=a$. With an image the
evaluation is the validation that $O_k$ performs at an odd depth, now also at
an even depth, where it can only add a failure; the result at an odd depth is
the opened image in both openers, and at an even depth, a formula position,
it is $0$ or $[\,]$ in $E_k$ and $0$ in $O_k$, all of which read $0=0$. In the
proof of \cref{cert:lem:leaves} the opening of the update at the image is
compared with $r$ as a tree; with $E_k$ that opening has the reading of the
opening by $O_k$, which is all that the proof uses.
\end{proof}

% ed.: the program of 477 bytes of head_conversion (code/compact477.py) is
% the matched control of three packages but had no row in the article; its
% soundness is recorded here from the lemma and cert:prop:header.
The program of $477$ bytes of \pkg{head\_conversion}
(\texttt{code/compact477.py}, $272/306/477$) is the four-field program of
\cref{cert:prop:header}, with the header $[A,0]$, the opener $E_k$ and the
empty array in place of the scalar $0$ as initial neutral resource and as
administrative major. The empty array reads $0=0$ at a formula position and
is false-valued, so the proofs of \cref{cert:prop:header,cert:prop:scalar}
apply with \cref{cert:lem:eager}; its accepted canonical goals are the closed
theorems of $\mathcal U$. It is the matched control of
\pkg{computation\_leaves}, \pkg{export\_specialization},
\pkg{476\_byte\_presentation} and \pkg{abstraction\_and\_refinements}.

\subsubsection*{Computation equations as leaves}

\begin{definition}[Computation candidate; direct-leaf protocols]\label{cert:def:leaf}
For raw fields $s,u,t$ and a floor $k$ put
\[
H_k(s,u,t)\defeq
\begin{cases}
\bigl[[[s],u,t],\ O_k(t;[s,u,t],3)\bigr], & s\text{ true-valued},\\
\bigl[[s,u,t],\ u\bigr], & s\text{ false-valued},
\end{cases}
\]
with $E_k$ in place of $O_k$ in the programs with the eager opener. A
\emph{direct-leaf} program is obtained from a program of this chapter that adds
computation hypotheses (the branch (4)(ii) of \cref{cert:def:base}) by
deleting that branch and inserting, after the entry scan and the membership
test of a call and after the removal of its packet, the test ``the goal equals
$H_k(s,u,t)$'', on whose success the call returns. No hypothesis and no
parameter is added by a computation.
\end{definition}

The candidate $H_k(s,u,t)$ has exactly the two shapes recognized by step (T2)
of \cref{cert:def:two-field}, with $n=s$ or $n=[s]$, $b=u$, $h=t$ and $r$ the
right side. \pkg{computation\_leaves} delivers three direct-leaf programs: a
global three-field program that reads the goal on the first line and one
packet per line (control: the global stream program of
\cref{cert:prop:stream}, $244/272/608$), a local three-field program of the
same framing (control: the local stream program of \pkg{fused\_iteration},
\cref{cert:prop:stream-rewrites}(a), $249/265/605$), and a four-field program
with the header $[A,0]$, the eager opener and the empty array as neutral
resource (control: the program of $477$ bytes of \pkg{head\_conversion}).

\begin{proposition}[Direct computation leaves]\label{cert:prop:leaf}
Let $P$ be one of these three programs, on inputs of integers and arrays.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item If a call of $P$ with goal $a$, context $g$ and floor $k$ returns, and
$a$ and the members of $g$ are closed at floor $k$, then the auxiliary reading
of $a$ is derivable in $\Teq$ from the readings of the members of $g$.
\item For every canonical closed public formula that is a theorem of
$\mathcal U$ there is a certificate that $P$ accepts with that goal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) By induction on the returning call, as in the proof of the soundness of
the control. The case of membership is unchanged. If the leaf test succeeds,
the goal, which the entry scan has validated at floor $k$ before the packet
was removed, is literally equal to $H_k(s,u,t)$; by \cref{cert:lem:leaves}
(with \cref{cert:lem:eager} for the eager opener) its reading is an instance
of \rulename{I0} or \rulename{IS}, derivable without assumptions. Every other
returning call passes through the logical branches of the control, which are
unchanged, so the induction step of the control's proof applies; the deleted
branch is the only one that used \cref{cert:lem:supply}. The order matters:
the entry scan precedes the comparison, so the comparison is made with a
validated tree; a program that compared an unvalidated goal could accept a
raw tree whose reading is not the computation instance.

(b) Take a derivation in $\mathcal U$ and compile it by the canonical compiler
of the control (\cref{cert:thm:base-complete} and its refinements in
\cref{cert:sec:later}), with the following change at the leaves \rulename{I0}
and \rulename{IS}: such a leaf is compiled to the single packet with fields
$(0,a,h)$, or $(n,a,h)$ for \rulename{IS} with a predecessor $n$ that is not
the literal $0$, and with a major field $0$ or $[\,]$ in the four-field
program; no packet is emitted for the continuation, since there is none. A
canonical goal equals $H_k$ of these fields, by \cref{cert:lem:canonical} for
the successor case. For \rulename{IS} with the predecessor $0$ put
$Z\defeq\It{0}{0}{v}{0}$; the leaf $Z=0$ and the leaf
$\It{\Suc Z}{a}{u}{h}=h\subst{\It{Z}{a}{u}{h}}{u}$, whose predecessor is not
the literal $0$, give the instance at $0$ by \rulename{SE} with the source $Z$,
which is not the literal $0$, the target $0$ and the whole equation as
template. The leaf test is evaluated before the logical dispatch at every
call, so it must be defined on canonical logical packets: the update field
$t$ of an elimination is a canonical template, whose opening at depth $3$
instead of $0$ raises the depth of every position by $3$, so that its
references remain in range, and the update fields of introductions and of
openings of a major are $0$. The leaf test can succeed at a call that the
compiled derivation continues; the stack is therefore built by recursion on
the derivation together with the state of the call (goal, context, floor),
and at each node the compiler evaluates the two tests that can close a call
before its logical step. If the goal is a member of the context, no packet is
emitted, as in \cref{cert:thm:base-complete}. Otherwise the packet of the last
inference is formed; if the leaf test succeeds with it, this packet alone is
emitted and the subderivations are discarded, the conclusion being a
computation instance; only otherwise are the stacks of the major and of the
continuation emitted after it. Both tests depend only on the goal, the context,
the floor and the packet, and the run is deterministic, so every call consumes
exactly the stack constructed for it, also when this stack is followed by the
stacks of later calls. An unused prefix remains only after the outermost call.
% ed.: the reports argue that an early leaf leaves an unused prefix. That holds
% only at the outermost call: inside a major call the remaining packets would be
% read by the continuation of the caller. The branch-aware compilation is the
% repair given by offset472 (OFFSET_PROOF.txt, section 6).
Names of parameters follow the allocation of the program.
\end{proof}

The proposition concerns the accepted canonical goals; certificates of the
control that add computation hypotheses must be translated, and the untrusted
translators of the package specialize the parameters allocated only by deleted
supplies to $0$ and replace each use of a supplied equation by a leaf. The
programs have $243/271/616$, $248/264/613$ and $270/308/474$
(\texttt{code/checker\_tokens.py}, \texttt{code/checker\_ast.py},
\texttt{code/checker\_bytes.py}). Since repeated uses of one computation are
now repeated leaves, a certificate can become longer; the package reports
inherited certificates that become shorter, for instance $1541$ packets for
an induction with a used hypothesis against $268$.

\pkg{whole\_json\_refinement} gives the two stream programs the whole-input
reader of \pkg{three\_field}: the removal of the last element of the parsed
array replaces the parsing of the next line. By the argument of
\cref{cert:prop:stream}, read in the other direction, the programs accept the
array $[p_r,\dots,p_1,A]$ exactly when the stream programs accept the lines
$A,p_1,\dots,p_r$, with the same run, and so accept the same canonical goals.
They have $248/277/615$ and $254/268/613$
(\texttt{code/checker\_tokens248.py}, \texttt{code/checker\_ast268.py}).
\pkg{orbit\_equality} delivers the same two texts again
(\texttt{checkers/whole\_json\_tokens.py}, \texttt{checkers/whole\_json\_ast.py},
byte for byte those of \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement}) and compares them with
the whole-input three-field programs $249/278/607$ and $255/269/605$ instead
of the stream programs.

\pkg{computation\_leaves} also gives native programs with direct leaves, a
goal-validated one ($334/384/600$, \texttt{code/checker\_native.py}, control
$334/380/596$) and one with the test of the printed form ($340/389/625$,
\texttt{code/checker\_strict.py}, control $340/385/621$). Removing the
supply branch exposes a truth-valued initializer field to the introduction:
with the mode test $\lvert a\rvert\bmod 2=u$, the value $u=1$ at a compound
matrix of arity $1$ or $3$ would select progress at a compound matrix, which
the native calculus does not have. The programs therefore test
$\lvert a\rvert\bmod 2=u=0$; they also reset the flag of the first opening
before the leaf test, so that the candidate is opened in the proof phase. An
earlier draft of $620$ bytes without the first change is not delivered. Both
programs are larger than their controls.

\subsubsection*{One terminal test for hypotheses and computations}

\begin{lemma}[Idempotent opening]\label{cert:lem:retraction}
If $O_k(a;\bot,d)$ is defined, then $O_k(O_k(a;\bot,d);\bot,d)=O_k(a;\bot,d)$,
and the two trees have the same reading.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By induction on $a$. An array keeps its arity, so its children are traversed
at the same depths by both openings. A scalar in $[k,d)$ is retained twice. An
out-of-range scalar at an odd depth consults the missing image and fails, so
it does not occur. At an even depth it becomes the scalar $0$, which the second
opening retains if $0\in[k,d)$ and otherwise replaces by $0$ again; this holds
at negative depths below arrays of arity at least $5$ as well. The statement
on readings is \cref{cert:lem:opening}(b).
\end{proof}

For the eager opener the no-image opening is the identity
(\cref{cert:lem:eager}).

\begin{definition}[Uniform terminal protocol]\label{cert:def:terminal}
A \emph{uniform terminal} program removes a packet at the entry of every call
and returns if $O_k(a;\bot,0)$ is a member of $g$ extended by the one element
$H_k(s,u,t)$ formed from the fields of that packet; the candidate is not
retained in the contexts of later calls. Otherwise it performs the logical
step of its control with that packet. A hypothesis leaf is given a dummy
packet, $[0,0,0]$ or $[[\,],0,0,0]$, whose candidate $[[0,0,0],0]$ is formed
without an opening.
\end{definition}

The calculus behind the protocol is the following. Let $\mathsf R$ be
$\mathcal U$ (\cref{cert:def:U}) with \rulename{Hyp}, \rulename{I0} and
\rulename{IS} replaced by the one rule \rulename{Leaf}: from a computation
instance $C$ (an instance of \rulename{I0} or \rulename{IS} in the current
scope) and $A\in\Gamma\cup\{C\}$ infer $\Gamma\proves A$.

\begin{theorem}[Shared leaf on open sequents]\label{cert:thm:shared-leaf}
For every context $\Gamma$ and formula $A$ of one scope,
$\Gamma\proves A$ in $\mathcal U$ if and only if $\Gamma\proves A$ in
$\mathsf R$, by translations that preserve the conclusion, the context, the
scope and the height of the derivation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
From $\mathcal U$ to $\mathsf R$, by induction on the derivation: a
computation leaf $C$ becomes \rulename{Leaf} with that $C$; a hypothesis leaf
becomes \rulename{Leaf} with $C\defeq(\It{0}{0}{u}{0}=0)$, which is available
in every scope; the other rules are kept with the translated premises. From
$\mathsf R$ to $\mathcal U$: a \rulename{Leaf} with $A\in\Gamma$ becomes
\rulename{Hyp}, and otherwise $A=C$ is a computation leaf. Leaves become
leaves, so heights are preserved, and no term is substituted.
\end{proof}

The argument applies to any calculus with a hypothesis rule and a family of
axioms that has one member in every scope. It removes no axiom.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform terminals]\label{cert:prop:terminal}
Let $P$ be a uniform terminal program obtained from one of the three
direct-leaf programs of \cref{cert:prop:leaf}. On inputs of integers and
arrays, \cref{cert:prop:leaf}(a) holds for $P$, and every canonical closed
public theorem of $\mathcal U$ has a certificate that $P$ accepts.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Soundness: if the opened goal $a'=O_k(a;\bot,0)$ is a member of $g$, the
control's argument applies. If $a'=H\defeq H_k(s,u,t)$, then by
\cref{cert:lem:retraction} the tree $H$ is validated at floor $k$, so by
\cref{cert:lem:leaves} its reading is a computation instance, and by
\cref{cert:lem:opening}(b) the reading of $a$ is the reading of $a'$, that is,
of $H$. With the eager opener $a'=a$. A failed construction of $H$ rejects. The
candidate enters no context, and the floor is decremented only in the
continuation, as in the control.

Completeness: compile as in \cref{cert:prop:leaf}(b) and insert a dummy packet
at every hypothesis leaf, and, in a program that proves every major by a call,
also at each neutral major, which is closed by membership of the neutral
resource and allocates no name. On canonical trees the no-image opening is the
identity, so the comparisons are those of \cref{cert:prop:leaf}. A match
earlier than the compiled leaf ends a sound branch; the compiler of
\cref{cert:prop:leaf}(b) emits at such a node the packet alone (here the dummy
packet when the goal is a member of the context), so that no packet of the
discarded subderivations is left for a later call.
% ed.: "the rest is unused" is replaced by the branch-aware compilation of
% cert:prop:leaf(b); see the comment there.
Translating a certificate of a local program into a global one renames the
parameters.
\end{proof}

The raw acceptance changes: the stream program of \pkg{computation\_leaves}
compares the raw goal and rejects the goal \texttt{[[0,[99,99],0],[0,0]]} with
the packet \texttt{[0,[0,0],0]}, while the uniform terminal program compares
its opening, in which the annotations $99$ at the even depth $8$ become $0$,
and accepts; the reading is the true equation $\It{0}{0}{u}{0}=0$.
\pkg{shared\_leaf} and \pkg{uniform\_terminals} deliver the same global stream
program ($242/268/565$) and the same four-field program ($272/306/469$); the
latter writes the selector as $\mathrm{any}((m,3\bmod\lvert a\rvert))$, which
is defined at every selector of a canonical run because a nonempty array goal
would otherwise have matched the neutral resource $[\,]$
(\cref{cert:prop:eager-selector}). Their local stream programs differ:
\pkg{shared\_leaf} writes the initial context $[0]$ as the byte string
\texttt{b'\textbackslash x00'}, whose iteration yields the integer $0$ and
which becomes a list at the first extension ($246/262/556$), and
\pkg{uniform\_terminals} initializes the global floor in a default argument of
the opener, evaluated once at its definition ($247/263/553$). Both stream
programs replace the loop of the control by a recursive call, and the local
programs check every major, including a neutral one, by a call. Terminal
packets make certificates longer. \pkg{shared\_leaf} records and rejects one
further shortcut, choosing the mode of the introduction from the arity of the
generated hypothesis: an auxiliary matrix with $Q$ at its root has arity $3$,
like the closure obtained by opening $D$, and would receive progress; no
counterexample is exhibited (\cref{cert:sec:questions}).

\pkg{fixed\_points\_and\_checker} rewrites the local stream program of
\pkg{shared\_leaf}. Call the latter $L$: its context is a list, initially the
byte string \texttt{b'\textbackslash x00'}, and its final call has the form
$c\bigl([{*}g,\ O(c(g,s)\lor s\cdot 3\lor(a\defeq O(a;t,2));\ \iota)],\ O(a;[k])\bigr)$
with $\iota\defeq(k\defeq k-1)\cdot(1\ \text{if}\ s\ \text{else}\ \lvert a\rvert=2)$,
where $s$ is the major after the replacement test. Let $L'$ be the program in
which the context is passed as the variadic tail $g$ of the arguments, the
initial context is the tuple $(0)$, the major call $c(s,{*}g)$ is a statement,
followed by $h\defeq s\cdot 3\lor(a\defeq O(a;t,2))$, the statement $k\defeq k-1$
and the final call $c(O(a;[k]),{*}g,O(h;\iota'))$ with
$\iota'\defeq(s\lor\lvert a\rvert=2)\land k$.

\begin{proposition}[Major call as a statement]\label{cert:prop:tuple-stream}
On inputs of integers and arrays, $L'$ accepts exactly the inputs that $L$
accepts, with the same records consumed and the same final floor. Hence it
satisfies \cref{cert:prop:terminal}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Call two raw trees \emph{related} if they become equal when every Boolean
\texttt{False} in them is replaced by the integer $0$. The value $\iota'$ is the
new floor $k$ when $s$ is true-valued or $\lvert a\rvert=2$, as $\iota$ is, and
otherwise \texttt{False} where $\iota$ is $0$; in both programs $\lvert a\rvert$
is evaluated only when $s$ is false-valued, at the same opened matrix. On
related arguments the opener succeeds or fails together and gives related
results: \texttt{False} and $0$ agree in every comparison, remainder, product,
truth value and attempted iteration, and an image \texttt{False} is retained as
an in-range scalar exactly where $0$ would be. Membership and equality of
arrays compare with \texttt{==}, under which related trees are equal. Contexts
of $L$ and $L'$ have the same members in the same order, the byte string
contributing the integer $0$, and no context is changed in place. Every
returning call returns the null value, so $c(g,s)\lor s\cdot 3$ is
$s\cdot 3$ after the same major call. Both programs make the major call, then
open the witness at the old floor, then decrement the floor once, then perform
the two openings of the final call at the new floor, in opposite orders; these
openings read the floor, assign nothing and do not read the input stream, so by
the argument of \cref{cert:prop:tail} they commute, and if one fails both
programs reject before a record is read. Induction on the consumed records
relates the states of the two runs call by call.
\end{proof}

The program (\texttt{code/checker\_tokens241.py}) has $241/267/566$ against
$246/262/556$; it reads the goal on the first line and one record per line and
carries the double dagger. Its package also lists the global stream program
$242/268/565$, which tests the major only when it is true-valued.

\pkg{ast261} and the compact part of \pkg{checked\_presentations} rewrite the
two other stream programs of \pkg{shared\_leaf}. Let $L$ be its stream program
with the byte-string context ($246/262/556$, \texttt{code/checker\_ast.py}) and
$G$ its global stream program ($242/268/565$, \texttt{code/checker\_tokens.py}).
In $G$ the final part of a call that does not close is
\[
h\defeq\bigl(c(s,{*}g)\lor 3s\ \text{if}\ s\ \text{else}\ (a\defeq O(a_0;t,2))\bigr),\qquad
k\defeq k-1,\qquad c\bigl(O(a;[k]),{*}g,O(h;\iota)\bigr),
\]
with $\iota\defeq(s\lor\lvert a\rvert=2)\land k$, where $s$ is the major after
the replacement test and $a_0$ the child of the goal.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $L_{\mathrm d}$ is $L$ with the statement $k\defeq 0$ before the definition
of the opener deleted and the default value $0$ of the depth parameter of the
opener written as the assignment expression $(k\defeq 0)$; this is the device
by which \pkg{uniform\_terminals} initializes its floor.
\item $G_{\mathrm b}$ is $G$ with the assignment of $h$ replaced by the
statement ``if $s$, call $c(s,{*}g)$; otherwise $a\defeq O(a_0;t,2)$'', and with
$h$ in the final call replaced by $3s\lor a$.
\item $G^{\mathrm w}$ and $G^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm b}$ are $G$ and $G_{\mathrm b}$
with the reading of the next line replaced by the removal of the last element of
one array parsed from the whole input.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}[Rewritings of the uniform terminal stream programs]\label{cert:prop:terminal-rewrites}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item On every input, $L_{\mathrm d}$ and $L$ perform the same operations after
the definition of the opener; they read the same lines and accept the same
inputs.
\item On inputs of integers and arrays, $G_{\mathrm b}$ and $G$ accept the same
inputs, read the same records and make the same calls, with the same goals,
contexts and floors.
\item $G^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm b}$ accepts the array $[p_r,\dots,p_1,A]$ exactly
when $G_{\mathrm b}$ accepts the lines $A,p_1,\dots,p_r$, with the same run, and
likewise $G^{\mathrm w}$ and $G$.
\end{enumerate}
Hence the five programs accept the canonical goals of \cref{cert:prop:terminal}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) The default values of a function are evaluated once, when its definition
is executed, in the enclosing module scope. The assignment expression therefore
binds the module name $k$ to the integer $0$ and has the value $0$, which
becomes the default depth, as in $L$. Before the definition no statement of
either program reads $k$, calls a function or reads the input. After it the
two modules have the same value of $k$, the same defaults $(0\mathrm{j},0)$ and
the same bodies, and the rest of the two texts is identical; induction on the
steps of the run gives the claim. The assignment expression is evaluated once;
moving $k\defeq 0$ into the body of the opener would reset the floor at every
opening and is a different program.

(b) Neither program has a return statement with a value, so every returning
call returns the null value. After the replacement test $s$ is false-valued
only if it is $0$ or $[\,]$, since the replacement sets it to a nonempty array.
If $s$ is true-valued, both programs make the same major call $c(s,{*}g)$; after
it returns, the old $h$ is $3s$, a nonzero integer or a nonempty array, so $3s\lor
a$ is $3s$. If $s$ is false-valued, neither makes a major call, both perform
$a\defeq O(a_0;t,2)$, the old $h$ is the new $a$, and $3s$ is $0$ or $[\,]$, so
$3s\lor a$ is $a$. Both then decrement the floor once. The only relocated
evaluation is that of $3s$, which now follows the decrement and the opening of
the new goal; it reads neither the floor nor the input, assigns nothing and is
defined on integers and arrays, so it changes no value and causes no failure,
and if the opening of the new goal fails, both programs reject before a further
record is read. Induction on the consumed records relates the two runs call by
call, in both directions.

(c) The argument of \cref{cert:prop:stream}, read in the other direction, as for
the programs of \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement}.
\end{proof}

The programs are $L_{\mathrm d}$ ($247/261/557$, \pkg{ast261},
\texttt{code/checker.py}), against $246/262/556$: the assignment statement (four
nodes) and the constant default (one node) give way to an assignment expression
of four nodes; $G_{\mathrm b}$ ($240/266/592$, \pkg{checked\_presentations},
\texttt{compact/code/checker240.py}, delivered again as
\texttt{code/compact\_stream.py}), against $242/268/565$; and
$G^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm b}$ ($245/272/591$, \texttt{compact/code/checker245\_whole.py},
again as \texttt{code/compact\_whole.py}), against $G^{\mathrm w}$
($247/274/564$, \texttt{compact/controls/checker247\_whole.py}), which the package
constructs by the change of framing and which no earlier package delivers. The
stream programs carry the double dagger. The whole-input programs read the
certificates of \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement}, with a packet at every call; the
line reader may leave an unread suffix, the whole-input reader parses the whole
array first. \pkg{checked\_presentations} also records, as rejected, a
computation candidate of $468$ bytes whose counter is a slice of the source; it
is the variant of \cref{cert:rem:examined-variants} that produces the counter
$[\,]$ for a zero computation.

The checker part of \pkg{scalar\_constancy} delivers $L_{\mathrm d}$ once more
(\texttt{checkers/code/checker\_ast261.py}, byte for byte the text of
\pkg{ast261}, against the control $L$, \texttt{checkers/controls/ast262.py}),
with the proof of (a), and delivers $T_{+}$ of \cref{cert:sec:offset}; neither
program is new.

\subsubsection*{Packets as arguments, structural binding and reservation at return}

This part follows \pkg{direct\_packets} and the stream programs that
\pkg{procedure\_presentation} includes unchanged from an earlier package,
\pkg{return\_reservation}, which is not otherwise represented in the article.
Let $T$ be the four-field uniform terminal program of \pkg{shared\_leaf} and
\pkg{uniform\_terminals} ($272/306/469$, \cref{cert:prop:terminal}), whose
checking function is an anonymous function $c(g,a,k)$ with the body
$[E\ \text{for}\ m,s,u,t\ \text{in}\ [q()]]$, where $q$ removes the last element
of the input and $E$ contains the major call in the conjunction
``$c(g,m,k)$ and $3m$''. Let $T_{\mathrm d}$ be $T$ with three changes. The
checking function takes the fields of its packet as arguments, $c(g,a,k,m,s,u,t)$,
and its body is $E$. Every recursive call passes ${*}q()$ as its last argument, so
that the packet of the callee is removed when the arguments of the call are
evaluated; the major call becomes $c(g,m,k,{*}q())$ and is used in the product
$c(g,m,k,{*}q())\cdot m\cdot 3$ in place of the conjunction. The root call is
$c([[\,]],{*}q())$, so that the last element of the input is $[A,0,m,s,u,t]$:
the header $[A,0]$ followed by the fields of the first packet. For an array
$\rho$ put
\[
F\bigl(\rho+[[m,s,u,t],[A,0]]\bigr)\defeq\rho+[[A,0,m,s,u,t]];
\]
$F$ is a bijection from the arrays whose last two elements have the lengths $4$
and $2$ onto the arrays whose last element has the length $6$.

\begin{proposition}[Packets as arguments]\label{cert:prop:direct-packets}
On inputs of integers and arrays, $T$ accepts $\sigma$ exactly when
$T_{\mathrm d}$ accepts $F(\sigma)$, and corresponding calls have equal goals,
contexts, floors and remaining stacks; every input that $T_{\mathrm d}$ accepts
lies in the image of $F$. Hence $T_{\mathrm d}$ accepts the canonical goals of
\cref{cert:prop:terminal}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(i) Every returning call of $T_{\mathrm d}$ returns the Boolean \texttt{True}: it
returns the value of a disjunction whose first operand is a membership test,
whose value is a Boolean, and whose second operand is the final call, which
returns \texttt{True} by induction on the tree of calls. A returning call of $T$
returns a one-element list, a true value.

(ii) The arguments of a call are evaluated from left to right, and ${*}q()$ is
the last of them, so the packet of a callee of $T_{\mathrm d}$ is removed after
its other arguments are evaluated and before the first step of its body. A
callee of $T$ removes its packet as the first step of its body, after the same
arguments. No operation between the two moments reads the input or changes a
value. At the root, $T$ removes $[A,0]$ and then $[m,s,u,t]$, and $T_{\mathrm d}$
removes $[A,0,m,s,u,t]$; an element of another length is rejected by both, by
the parameter list of the root call in $T$ and by the seven positional
parameters, none of them optional, in $T_{\mathrm d}$. A packet of a length other
than $4$, or a scalar in place of a packet, is rejected by the destructuring in
$T$ and by the binding of the parameters in $T_{\mathrm d}$.

(iii) After a returning major call the product is $\mathtt{True}\cdot m\cdot 3$,
which equals $3m$ for an integer $m$ and is a copy of $3m$ for an array $m$; the
conjunction of $T$ has the value $3m$ by (i). No operation of either program
tests the identity of arrays or changes an array in place, so the copy is not
observable. A failed major call fails in both.

Relate a state of $T$ after the removal of the header with the state of
$T_{\mathrm d}$ after the removal of the merged record, and corresponding calls
with equal arguments and equal remaining stacks. By (ii) and (iii) related calls
perform the same openings, tests, assignments and recursive calls in the same
order, and an operation fails in one exactly when it fails in the other.
Induction on the tree of calls in both directions gives the claim; both
programs print the same message after the root call returns.
\end{proof}

The program (\texttt{code/checkers/checker.py}) has $274/303/465$, against
$272/306/469$ for $T$ (\texttt{code/checkers/control.py}, the text of
\pkg{shared\_leaf}). Its certificates differ from those of $T$ only in the last
element; the package's translator implements $F$ and its inverse and is not used
in the proof.

\pkg{direct\_packets} also rewrites the stream programs $L$ and $L'$. Let
$L_{\mathrm m}$ be $L$ with the destructuring ``$s,u,t=r=$ the next record''
replaced by the statement ``match the next record: case $[s,u,t]$ as $r$: pass'',
with the rest of the body unchanged; let $L^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm m}$ and
$L'^{\mathrm w}$ be $L_{\mathrm m}$ and $L'$ with the reading of the next line
replaced by the removal of the last element of one array parsed from the whole
input.

\begin{proposition}[Structural binding]\label{cert:prop:match}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Executed by CPython 3.12.14 on inputs whose records are integers and
arrays, $L_{\mathrm m}$ and $L$ accept the same inputs, read the same lines and
make the same calls, with the same goals, contexts and floors.
\item $L^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm m}$ accepts the array $[p_r,\dots,p_1,A]$ exactly
when $L_{\mathrm m}$ accepts the lines $A,p_1,\dots,p_r$, with the same run, and
likewise $L'^{\mathrm w}$ and $L'$.
\end{enumerate}
Hence the three programs accept the canonical goals of \cref{cert:prop:terminal}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) For an array of length $3$ the sequence pattern with three capture patterns
succeeds and binds $s,u,t$ to its entries and $r$ to the array itself, as the
destructuring does. For an integer, or an array of another length, the
destructuring of $L$ fails and $L$ rejects. The pattern fails as well, and in
the measured runtime the test that the subject is a sequence of length $3$
precedes every binding of a capture name, which the package checks in the
compiled code; so $s,u,t,r$, which are local names of the function because the
body binds them, remain unbound in the new frame of the call. The next step
evaluates the opening of the goal and then the tuple of the membership test,
whose last member is formed from $s$; reading the unbound name fails, so
$L_{\mathrm m}$ rejects before it reads a further record or returns. After a
successful match every later step is that of $L$. Induction on the consumed
records gives the claim. (b) The argument of \cref{cert:prop:stream}, read in
the other direction, as for the programs of \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement}.
\end{proof}

The language reference leaves unspecified which names a failed pattern has
bound, so (a) is a statement about the measured runtime, as the package states;
on certificates that consist of three-field records the two programs agree in
every runtime. The programs are $L'^{\mathrm w}$
(\texttt{code/checkers/checker\_tokens\_json.py}, $246/273/565$),
$L^{\mathrm w}_{\mathrm m}$ (\texttt{code/checkers/checker\_ast\_json.py},
$258/263/595$) and $L_{\mathrm m}$ (\texttt{code/checkers/checker\_ast\_jsonl.py},
$252/259/595$), against $L'$ ($241/267/566$) and $L$ ($246/262/556$), reproduced
with identical text. In the syntax tree the capture names of a pattern are
fields of the pattern and not separate nodes, which saves three nodes against
$L$ and costs six tokens and $39$ bytes. The package compares the two
whole-input programs with those of \pkg{whole\_json\_refinement} ($248/277/615$
and $254/268/613$).

The second device changes when a fresh name is allocated. Write the final call
of a call of $L$ that does not close as
\[
c\bigl([{*}g,\ O(c(g,s)\lor 3s\lor(a\defeq O(a;t,2));\ \iota)],\ O(a;[k])\bigr),
\qquad \iota\defeq(k\defeq k-1)\cdot(1\ \text{if}\ s\ \text{else}\ \lvert a\rvert=2),
\]
as in \cref{cert:prop:tuple-stream}. Let $L_{\mathrm r}$ be $L$ with the final call
\[
c\bigl([{*}g,\ O(3s\lor(a\defeq O(a;t,2));\ c(g,s)\lor k\cdot\mathrm{bool}(s\lor\lvert a\rvert=2))],\ O(a;[k])\bigr)
\]
and with the statement $k\defeq k-1$ at the end of every call, after its
terminal test or after its final call. So the threefold repetition of the major,
or in an introduction the opening of the matrix at the witness, is formed before
the major call, and the fresh name is the floor left by the return of the major
call. Let $L'_{\mathrm r}$ be $L_{\mathrm r}$ with the floor initialized as in
$L_{\mathrm d}$, with every call returning the floor after its final decrement,
and with the image of the hypothesis written $c(g,s)\cdot(1\ \text{if}\ s\
\text{else}\ \lvert a\rvert=2)$.

\begin{proposition}[Reservation of the fresh name at return]\label{cert:prop:return-reservation}
Let $P$ be $L_{\mathrm r}$ or $L'_{\mathrm r}$, on inputs of integers and arrays.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item If a call of $P$ with goal $a$ and context $g$ returns, and $a$ and the
members of $g$ are closed at the floor at the entry of the call, then the
auxiliary reading of $a$ is derivable in $\Teq$ from the readings of the members
of $g$.
\item Every canonical closed public theorem of $\mathcal U$ has a stream
certificate that $P$ accepts.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The floor is changed only by the final decrement of a call, so it never
increases, and if a call entered at the floor $k_0$ returns, the floor at its
return is at most $k_0-1$. In $L_{\mathrm r}$ every returning call returns the
null value, so the image is $k\cdot\mathrm{bool}(s\lor\lvert a\rvert=2)$, read
after the major call; in $L'_{\mathrm r}$ the major call returns the floor at its
return, and the product with $1$ or with a Boolean is the same value. The
initialization of $L'_{\mathrm r}$ is that of \cref{cert:prop:terminal-rewrites}(a).

(a) By induction on the returning call. Let $k_0$ be the floor at the entry. The
opening of the goal, the terminal test, the replacement test with the opening of
the source instance, and in an introduction the opening of the matrix at the
witness, are evaluated at $k_0$; the terminal cases are those of
\cref{cert:prop:terminal}, and the final decrement changes no reading. Otherwise
the major call is made with the context $g$ at the floor $k_0$, as in $L$, and
the induction hypothesis applies to it as in the proof for $L$. When it has
